Comparing Fock spaces in types A ( 1 ) A^{(1)} and A ( 2 ) A^{(2)}
Matthew Fayers

Abstract
We compare the canonical bases of level- 1 1 quantised Fock spaces in affine types A ( 1 ) A^{(1)} and A ( 2 ) A^{(2)} , showing how to derive the canonical basis in type A 2 ​ n ( 2 ) A^{(2)}_{2n} from the the canonical basis in type A n ( 1 ) A^{(1)}_{n} in certain weight spaces. In particular, we derive an explicit formula for the canonical basis in extremal weight spaces, which correspond to RoCK blocks of double covers of symmetric groups. In a forthcoming paper with Kleshchev and Morotti we will use this formula to find the decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks of double covers with abelian defect.



2020 Mathematics subject classification: 17B37,05E10,20C25,20C30

1
Introduction

This paper is motivated by the
decomposition number problem
for the symmetric groups and their double covers in characteristic
p
. Although a solution to this problem seems a long way off, several important results are known. One of these results gives the decomposition numbers for
RoCK blocks
of symmetric groups; these are particularly well understood blocks which have been used in a variety of applications. The formula for the decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks in the abelian defect case was given by Chuang and Tan
[
CT2
]
, and results of Turner
[
T
]
allow these results to be extended to RoCK blocks with non-abelian defect groups.

It is natural to seek analogous results for the double cover
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
of the symmetric group (which controls projective representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
). The case of characteristic
2
behaves very differently (and is dealt with in
[
F3
]
), so we concentrate here on odd characteristic. The representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
which do not descend to representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are called
spin
representations of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and the blocks of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
containing spin representations are called spin blocks. RoCK blocks for symmetric groups can be characterised as elements of the maximal equivalence class of blocks under the Scopes equivalence
[
Sco1
]
on blocks of symmetric groups. The Scopes–Kessar equivalence
[
Ke
]
for spin blocks of double covers suggests a natural analogue of RoCK blocks, and this can be realised in a combinatorial way using the abacus. These blocks have recently been studied in detail by Kleshchev and Livesey
[
KlL
]
, who prove Broué’s abelian defect group conjecture for RoCK blocks. This has been used even more recently by Brundan and Kleshchev, and independently Ebert, Lauda and Vera, to show that Broué’s conjecture holds for all spin blocks
[
BK
,
ELV
]
. However, the results of Kleshchev and Livesey do not directly address the decomposition number problem for spin RoCK blocks, and this is the main focus here.

In this paper we address RoCK blocks by studying quantum algebra. Let
U=U_{q}(A^{(1)}_{p-1})
denote the quantised universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra of type
A^{(1)}_{p-1}
. The
level-
1
Fock space
is a highest-weight
U
-module with a simple combinatorial construction in terms of integer partitions. The submodule generated by a highest-weight vector is isomorphic to the irreducible highest-weight module
V(\Lambda_{0})
, so the Fock space provides a combinatorial framework for studying
V(\Lambda_{0})
; this approach has proved useful, for example, in constructing the crystals.
V(\Lambda_{0})
possess an important basis called the
canonical basis
, which provides a connection to representation theory of symmetric groups and Iwahori–Hecke algebras, via the work of Lascoux–Leclerc–Thibon
[
LLT
]
and Ariki
[
A
]
, who showed that decomposition numbers for Hecke algebras of type
A
in characteristic zero can be obtained by specialising canonical basis coefficients at
q=1
. This means in particular that these decomposition numbers can be calculated algorithmically. A further conjecture due to James suggested that the same should apply for decomposition numbers of symmetric groups, in blocks with abelian defect groups. James’s conjecture is now known to be false in general
[
W
]
, but there are a wide variety of situations where it is known to hold, and it has provided important inspiration for results on decomposition numbers. In particular, the formula due to Chuang and Tan
[
CT2
]
for decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
was inspired by their earlier calculation of the canonical basis in weight spaces corresponding to RoCK blocks, and shows in particular that James’s conjecture holds for RoCK blocks.

An analogous connection to quantum groups for spin representations was found by Leclerc and Thibon
[
LT
]
, using the quantum group of type
A^{(2)}_{p-1}
. This quantum group also acts on a combinatorially defined level-
1
Fock space (now defined in terms of
p
-strict partitions), which possesses an irreducible highest-weight submodule with a canonical basis. Leclerc and Thibon formulated an analogue of James’s conjecture for spin representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. This conjecture is also known not to hold in general, but it does hold in many special cases, in particular all known cases of decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks. Motivated by this conjecture, the aim of the present paper is to find the canonical bases for the weight spaces of the basic
U_{q}(A^{(2)}_{p-1})
-module corresponding to spin RoCK blocks. Rather than directly determining the canonical basis, we deduce our result from the results of Chuang–Tan by proving more general results comparing the canonical bases in types
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
: we show that if
\beta
is a restricted
p
-strict partition satisfying a particular additional condition which says that the
p
-bar-core of
\beta
is large in a certain specific sense relative to the sum of the parts of
\beta
divisible by
p
, then we can obtain the canonical basis element labelled by
\beta
from a corresponding canonical basis element in type
A^{(1)}
by an adjustment involving inverse Kostka polynomials. In certain cases this allows the canonical bases for entire weight spaces to be computed, including weight spaces corresponding to RoCK blocks. Combining this result with the Chuang–Tan formula for RoCK blocks in type
A^{(1)}
yields our main result (
8.2
).

Combining our theorem with the Leclerc–Thibon conjecture (specialised to the case of RoCK blocks), we arrive at a conjecture for the decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks (
9.1
). In a forthcoming paper with Kleshchev and Morotti
[
FKM
]
we will prove this conjecture.

Acknowledgement
.

This research was partly supported by EPSRC Small Grant EP/W005751/1.

2
Background

In this section we set out some background details on partitions and Fock spaces.

2.1
Elementary notation

We write
\mathbb{N}
for the set of positive integers and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the set
\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
consists of cosets
a+m\mathbb{Z}=\left\{\left.a+mb\ \right|\ \smash{b\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\}
. Given any set
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
B+a=\left\{\left.b+a\ \right|\ \smash{b\in B}\right\}
.

2.2
Partitions

A
partition
is an infinite weakly decreasing sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
of non-negative integers which is eventually zero. When writing partitions, we omit the trailing zeroes and group together equal parts with a superscript. The partition
(0,0,\dots)
is written as
\varnothing
. If
\lambda
is a partition, the integers
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots
are called the
parts
of
\lambda
. We write
\lvert\lambda\rvert=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots
, and we say that
\lambda
is a partition of
\lvert\lambda\rvert
. The
length

l(\lambda)
is the number of non-zero parts of
\lambda
. We write
\mathcal{P}
for the set of all partitions.

If
\lambda
is a partition and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
n\lambda
is defined to be the partition
(n\lambda_{1},n\lambda_{2},\dots)
. If
\lambda
and
\mu
are partitions, then
\lambda\sqcup\mu
is defined to be the partition of
\lvert\lambda\rvert+\lvert\mu\rvert
obtained by combining all the parts of
\lambda
and
\mu
in decreasing order.

The
Young diagram
of
\lambda
is the set

\left\{\left.(r,c)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\ \right|\ \smash{c\leqslant\lambda_{r}}\right\}

whose elements are called the
nodes
of
\lambda
. In general, a node means an element of
\mathbb{N}^{2}
. We use the English convention for drawing Young diagrams, in which
r
increases down the page and
c
increases from left to right. We abuse notation by identifying
\lambda
with its Young diagram; so for example we may write
\lambda\subseteq\mu
to mean that
\lambda_{r}\leqslant\mu_{r}
for all
r
.

If
\lambda
is a partition, the
conjugate
partition
\lambda^{\prime}
is the partition obtained by reflecting the Young diagram of
\lambda
in the main diagonal; that is,
\lambda^{\prime}_{r}=\lvert\left\{\left.c\in\mathbb{N}\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda_{c}\geqslant r}\right\}\rvert
.

The
dominance order
on partitions is defined by writing
\lambda\trianglerighteqslant\mu
(and saying that
\lambda

dominates

\mu
) if
\lvert\lambda\rvert=\lvert\mu\rvert
and
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{r}\geqslant\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{r}
for all
r
.

We say that a node
(r,c)
of
\lambda
is
removable
if it can be removed from
\lambda
to leave the Young diagram of a partition (that is, if
c=\lambda_{r}>\lambda_{r+1}
), and we write the resulting partition as
\lambda\setminus(r,c)
. Similarly, a node
(r,c)
not in
\lambda
is an
addable node
of
\lambda
if it can be added to
\lambda
to give a partition, and we write this partition as
\lambda\cup(r,c)
.

Now fix an integer
m\geqslant 2
. We say that a partition
\lambda
is
m
-restricted
if
\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}<m
for all
r
. We define the
residue
of the node
(r,c)
to be
c-r+m\mathbb{Z}
. Given
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
, we use the term
i
-addable node
to mean “addable node of residue
i
”, and we define the term
i
-removable
similarly. If
\lambda
and
\mu
are partitions, we write
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mu
to mean that
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by adding
r
nodes of residue
i
. (In the case
r=1
, we just write
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mu
.)

We also need to define rim hooks and
m
-cores. The
rim
of a partition
\lambda
is the set of nodes
(r,c)
of
\lambda
for which
(r+1,c+1)
is not a node of
\lambda
. A
rim
m
-hook
of
\lambda
is a set of
m
consecutive nodes of the rim which can be removed to leave the Young diagram of a smaller partition. The
m
-core
of
\lambda
is the partition obtained by repeatedly removing rim
m
-hooks from
\lambda
until none remain, and the
m
-weight
of
\lambda
is the number of rim hooks removed to reach the
m
-core.

It is convenient in combinatorial representation theory to depict partitions using the abacus. Keeping
m
fixed as above, we draw an abacus with
m
vertical runners labelled
0,\dots,m-1
from left to right. We mark positions on the runners, labelled
0,1,2,\dots
from left to right along successive rows from top to bottom.

Now given a partition
\lambda
, we choose a large integer
s
, and define the
beta-set

\mathcal{B}_{s}(\lambda)=\left\{\left.\lambda_{r}+s-r\ \right|\ \smash{1\leqslant r\leqslant s}\right\}.

Now draw the
s
-bead abacus display for
\lambda
by placing beads on the abacus in the positions corresponding to all the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{s}(\lambda)
. In an abacus display, we will say that a position is
occupied
if there is a bead at that position, and
empty
or
unoccupied
otherwise.

For example, suppose
m=4
and
\lambda=(8,7,5^{2},2,1^{3})
. Choosing
s=9
, we obtain the following abacus display. (Whenever we draw an abacus display, we adopt the convention that all positions below those depicted are empty.)

The abacus display for a partition is very useful in two ways.

(1)

Given
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
, let
a\in\{0,\dots,e-1\}
be such that
i=a-s+m\mathbb{Z}
. Then
i
-addable nodes of
\lambda
correspond to empty positions
b
on runner
a
such that position
b-1
is occupied (or
b=0
), while
i
-removable nodes correspond to occupied positions
b\geqslant 1
on runner
a
for which position
b-1
is empty.

(2)

If position
b\geqslant m
is occupied while position
b-m
is empty, then moving the bead from position
b
to position
b-m
corresponds to removing a rim
m
-hook from
\lambda
. This means in particular that the abacus display for the
m
-core of
\lambda
can be obtained by moving all the beads up their runners as far as they will go, and the number of bead moves needed to do this is the
m
-weight of
\lambda
.

We end with two combinatorial lemmas we will need later on. Given
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we write
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
to mean that
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by adding
r
nodes in different columns. We want to interpret this condition in terms of beta-sets; the next
Lemma
follows easily from the definitions.

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(1)

\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
if and only if there is a set
A\subset\mathbb{N}
with
\lvert A\rvert=r
such that
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by adding a part equal to
a
for each
a\in A
and then removing a part equal to
a-1
for each
a\in A
.

(2)

Suppose
s\in\mathbb{N}
is large. Then
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
if and only if there is a set
A\subset\mathbb{N}
with
\lvert A\rvert=r
and
A\cap\mathcal{B}_{s}(\lambda)=\emptyset
such that
\mathcal{B}_{s}(\mu)=\mathcal{B}_{s}(\lambda)\cup A\setminus(A-1)
.

We will also need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
with
\pi\neq\varnothing
. Let
k
equal the last non-zero part of
\pi
, and let
\pi^{-}
be the partition obtained by removing this last part. If
\sigma,\rho\in\mathcal{P}
with
\sigma\trianglerighteqslant\pi^{-}
and
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
, then
\rho\trianglerighteqslant\pi
, with equality only if
\sigma=\pi^{-}
.

Let
\sigma^{+}
be the partition obtained from
\sigma
by adding a node in each of the first
k
columns. Since
\rho
is obtained from
\sigma
by adding nodes in some
k
different columns, it follows that
\rho\trianglerighteqslant\sigma^{+}
. Also, from the definition of the dominance order (and the fact that conjugation of partitions reverses the dominance order) the condition
\sigma\trianglerighteqslant\pi^{-}
implies that
\sigma^{+}\trianglerighteqslant\pi
. So
\rho\trianglerighteqslant\pi
, and in order to get equality we need
\sigma^{+}=\pi
, which is the same as
\sigma=\pi^{-}
.
∎

2.3
h
-strict partitions

Now suppose
h\geqslant 3
is odd. We say that a partition is
h
-strict
if there is no
r
for which
\lambda_{r}=\lambda_{r+1}\not\equiv 0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
. We write
\mathcal{P}^{(h)}
for the set of all
h
-strict partitions.
We will generally use letters near the start of the Greek alphabet for
h
-strict partitions. We define the
bar-residue
of a node
(r,c)
to be the smaller of the residues of
c-1
and
h-c
modulo
h
; so the bar-residue of a node depends only on the column in which it lies, and the bar-residues follow the pattern

\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\cdots}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\frac{h-1}{2}}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\frac{h+1}{2}}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\frac{h-1}{2}}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\cdots}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{\cdots}

from left to right. A node of an
h
-strict partition
\lambda
is
bar-removable
if it can be removed from
\lambda
, possibly together with some other nodes of the same bar-residue, to leave an
h
-strict partition. Given
i\in\{0,\dots,\frac{h+1}{2}\}
, an
i
-bar-removable
node means a bar-removable node of bar-residue
i
. We define bar-addable and
i
-bar-addable nodes in a similar way. Note the distinction between removable nodes and bar-removable nodes. For example, if
h=5
and
\alpha=(6,2,1)
, then
(2,2)
is removable but not bar-removable, while
(1,5)
is bar-removable but not removable. This can be seen from the following diagram, in which we label the nodes with their bar-residues.

\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{0}

If
\alpha
and
\beta
are
h
-strict partitions, we write
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\beta
to mean that
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha
by adding
r
nodes of bar-residue
i
. (In the case
r=1
, we just write
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\beta
.)

Now we define the analogue of
m
-cores. If
\alpha
is an
h
-strict partition, then removing an
h
-bar from
\lambda
means either

\diamond

replacing a part
\alpha_{r}\geqslant h
with
\alpha_{r}-h
(provided either
\alpha_{r}\equiv 0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
or
\alpha_{r}-h
is not a part of
\alpha
) and reordering, or

\diamond

deleting two parts which sum to
h
.

The
h
-bar-core
of
\alpha
is the
h
-strict partition obtained by repeatedly removing
h
-bars until it is not possible to remove any more, and the
h
-bar-weight
of
\alpha
is the number of
h
-bars removed.

We also use abacus displays for
h
-strict partitions. In this paper, we use the convention employed by Kleshchev and Livesey
[
KlL
]
(a different version of the abacus for
h
-strict partitions was introduced in
[
Y
,
F4
]
). We take an abacus with
h
vertical runners labelled
0,\dots,h-1
from left to right, with positions
0,1,2,\dots
marked from left to right along successive rows. Given an
h
-strict partition
\alpha
, we place a bead on the abacus at position
\alpha_{r}
for each
r
. In particular, on runner
0
there can be more than one bead in a given position, and we regard position
0
as containing infinitely many beads. We call the resulting configuration the
bar-abacus display
for
\alpha
. We will use white beads when drawing bar-abacus displays, and we will decorate a bead on runner
0
with
a\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
to indicate that there are
a
beads at that position.

For example, if
h=5
and
\alpha=(18,12,10^{2},9,7,6,2)
, we obtain the following bar-abacus display.

\infty
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}

As with abacus displays, bar-abacus displays are useful for visualising some of the combinatorial concepts described above for
h
-strict partitions.

(1)

Suppose
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then
i
-bar-addable nodes of
\alpha
correspond to empty positions
b
on runner
i+1
or runner
h-i
for which position
b-1
is occupied, and
i
-bar-removable nodes correspond to occupied positions
b
on these runners for which position
b-1
is empty. A similar but more complicated statement holds for
i=0
.

(2)

Removing an
h
-bar from
\alpha
corresponds to either moving a bead from position
b\geqslant h
to position
b-h
(which must be empty if
b\not\equiv 0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
), or removing beads from positions
i
and
h-i
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

2.4
The Fock space in type
A^{(1)}

Now we introduce some quantum algebra. To begin with, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
and an indeterminate
x
, we define the quantum integer
[r]_{x}=(x^{r}-x^{-r})/(x-x^{-1})
and the quantum factorial
[r]^{!}_{x}=[r]_{x}[r-1]_{x}\dots[1]_{x}
.

Now fix an integer
m\geqslant 2
and an indeterminate
q
. We define
U_{m}
to be the quantum group
U_{q^{2}}(A_{m-1}^{(1)})
defined over
\mathbb{C}(q)
, with standard generators
\mathrm{e}_{i},\mathrm{f}_{i},\mathrm{t}_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}
. We define
U^{-}_{m}
to be the negative part of
U_{m}
, generated by
\left\{\left.\mathrm{f}_{i}\ \right|\ \smash{i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}}\right\}
. Note in particular that we define
U_{m}
with quantum parameter
q^{2}
rather than
q
; this will makes the comparison with the Fock space of type
A^{(2)}
easier.

We will be working with the level
1
Fock space for
U_{m}
, which we denote
\mathcal{F}
. This was introduced by Hayashi
[
Ha
]
, but we use the combinatorial description given by Mathas in
[
Mat
]
. The Fock space
\mathcal{F}
is a
\mathbb{C}(q)
-vector space with the set
\mathcal{P}
of all partitions as a basis, which we call the
standard basis
. We write
(\ ,\ )
for the inner product on
\mathcal{F}
for which the standard basis elements are orthonormal.

Each
\lambda
is a weight vector, i.e. a simultaneous eigenvector for the generators
\mathrm{t}_{i}
. We will not need to describe the weights explicitly, but we note that two standard basis vectors
\lambda
and
\mu
have the same weight if and only if
\lambda
and
\mu
have the same
m
-core and
m
-weight.

For the purposes of this paper we only need to describe the action of the divided powers
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(r)}=\mathrm{f}_{i}^{r}/[r]^{!}_{q^{2}}
on the standard basis. Recall that we write
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mu
if
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by adding
r
nodes of residue
i
. If this is the case,
n(\lambda,\mu)
to be the sum, over all nodes
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mu\setminus\lambda
, of the number of
i
-addable nodes of
\mu
to the left of
\mathfrak{a}
minus the number of
i
-removable nodes of
\lambda
to the left of
\mathfrak{a}
. Now the action of
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(r)}
on
\mathcal{F}
is defined by

\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(r)}\lambda=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathcal{P}\\
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mu\end{subarray}}q^{2n(\lambda,\mu)}\mu.

(Note in particular the factor of
2
, which arises because we take the defining quantum parameter of
U_{m}
to be
q^{2}
, not
q
.)

We will often read the coefficient
n(\lambda,\mu)
from the abacus display for
\lambda
. To do this, we recall from above that on an
s
-bead abacus display for
\lambda
, the addable and removable
i
-nodes can be read by looking at runners
a
and
a-1
, where
i=a-s+n\mathbb{Z}
; addable and removable nodes further to the left correspond to positions higher up these runners.

For example, suppose
m=3
and
\lambda=(6,5,4,1^{2})
, and take
i=1+3\mathbb{Z}
and
r=2
. Then
\lambda
has three
i
-addable nodes
(2,6)
,
(4,2)
and
(6,1)
, and one
i
-removable node
(3,4)
. So we obtain

\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(2)}\lambda=(6,5,4,2,1^{2})+(6^{2},4,1^{3})+q^{2}(6^{2},4,2,1).

We can see this calculation from the following abacus displays, where
i
-addable and -removable nodes can be seen on the two leftmost runners.

\lambda&(6,5,4,2,1^{2})&(6^{2},4,1^{3})&(6^{2},4,2,1)\end{array}

Now we come to the canonical basis. Let
\mathcal{V}_{m}
denote the
U^{-}_{m}
-submodule generated by
\varnothing
(which is in fact also the
U_{m}
-submodule generated by
\varnothing
). This is an irreducible highest-weight module for
U_{m}
with highest weight
\Lambda_{0}
, and admits an important involution
v\mapsto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v}
called the
bar involution
. This is
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
-linear, and can be defined on
\mathcal{V}_{m}
by the properties

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\varnothing,\qquad\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\mathrm{f}_{i}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v}\text{ for $i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mathbb{Z}$}.

We say that an element
v\in\mathcal{V}_{m}
is
bar-invariant
if
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v}=v
. The above properties imply that any
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
-linear combination of vectors of the form
\mathrm{f}_{i_{1}}\dots\mathrm{f}_{i_{r}}\varnothing
is bar-invariant.

The bar involution allows us to define the
canonical basis
of
\mathcal{V}_{m}
, which is the main object of study in this paper. This basis is written
\left\{\left.\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)\ \right|\ \smash{\mu\text{ an $m$-restricted partition}}\right\}
, and the canonical basis vectors
\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)
have the following properties (which are sufficient to define them uniquely).

\diamond

\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)
is bar-invariant.

\diamond

\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)
has the form
\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}\lambda
, where
\operatorname{d}_{\mu\mu}=1
and
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
is a polynomial divisible by
q^{2}
for
\lambda\neq\mu
.

\diamond

If
\lambda
and
\mu
are partitions and
\mu
is
m
-restricted, then
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}=0
unless
\lambda\trianglerighteqslant\mu
and
\lambda
has the same
m
-core as
\mu
.

In particular, the last condition ensures that
\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)
is a weight vector in
\mathcal{V}_{m}
, so we can talk about the canonical basis of a given weight space.

The coefficients
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
are called
q
-decomposition numbers
(or in our case
q^{2}
-decomposition numbers), in view of Ariki’s theorem that their evaluations at
q=1
yield decomposition numbers for Iwahori–Hecke algebras at an
m
th root of unity in
\mathbb{C}
. (Of course,
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
depends on the choice of
m
, but we will always make it clear from the context which value of
m
is intended.)

2.5
The Fock space in type
A^{(2)}

Now let
h\geqslant 3
be an odd integer, and set
n=\frac{1}{2}(h-1)
. Define
\check{U}_{h}
to be the quantum group
U_{q}(A_{h-1}^{(2)})
. We write the standard generators for
\check{U}
as
\check{\mathrm{e}}_{i},\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i},\check{\mathrm{t}}_{i}
, for
i\in\{0,\dots,n\}
. We define
\check{U}^{-}_{h}
to be the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-subalgebra generated by
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0},\dots,\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}
.

We let
\check{\mathcal{F}}
denote the level
1
Fock space for
\check{U}
. This is a
\mathbb{C}(q)
-vector space with the set
\mathcal{P}^{(h)}
of
h
-strict partitions as its standard basis. As in type
A^{(1)}
, we write
(\ ,\ )
for the inner product with respect to which this basis is orthonormal. Each
\alpha
is a weight vector, and two vectors
\alpha
and
\beta
have the same weight if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
have the same
h
-bar-core and
h
-bar-weight.

The action of
\check{U}_{h}
on
\check{\mathcal{F}}
is more complicated than for
\mathcal{F}
. We take the definitions from the paper
[
LT
]
by Leclerc and Thibon, with additional detail from the author’s paper
[
F4
]
. (Note that we use the more standard labelling of the Dynkin diagram in type
A^{(2)}_{h-1}
, so that Leclerc and Thibon’s generators
\mathrm{f}_{0},\dots,\mathrm{f}_{n}
are our
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n},\dots,\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}
).

Given
i\in\{0,\dots,n\}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}^{(r)}=\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}^{r}/[r]^{!}_{q_{i}}
, where

q_{i}=\begin{cases}q&\text{if }i=0\\
q^{2}&\text{if }0<i<n\\
q^{4}&\text{if }i=n.\end{cases}

Recall that we write
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\beta
if
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha
by adding
r
nodes of bar-residue
i
. If this is the case, we define
\check{n}(\alpha,\beta)
to be the sum, over all nodes
\mathfrak{a}
of
\beta\setminus\alpha
, of the number of
i
-bar-addable nodes of
\beta
to the left of
\mathfrak{a}
minus the number of
i
-bar-removable nodes of
\alpha
to the left of
\mathfrak{a}
. Further, if
i=0
, let
M
be the set of integers
m\geqslant 1
such that column
mh+1
contains a node of
\beta\setminus\alpha
but column
mh
does not. For each
m\in M
, let
b_{m}
be the number of times
mh
occurs as a part of
\alpha
, and set
N=\prod_{m\in M}(1-(-q^{2})^{b_{m}})
. (If
i\neq 0
, then set
N=1
.)

Now the action of
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}
is given by

\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}^{(r)}\alpha=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathcal{P}^{(h)}\\
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i:r}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\beta\end{subarray}}Nq_{i}^{\check{n}(\alpha,\beta)}\beta.

As in type
A^{(1)}
, we will often read the coefficient
\check{n}(\alpha,\beta)
from the bar-abacus displays for
\alpha
and
\beta
; here the situation is slightly more complicated when
i<n
, since there are more than two runners to consider.

Now we can define the canonical basis; this is done in essentially the same way as in type
A^{(1)}
. Let
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{h}
denote the
\check{U}^{-}_{h}
-submodule generated by
\varnothing
(which is also the
\check{U}_{h}
-submodule generated by
\varnothing
). This is an irreducible module with highest weight
\Lambda_{0}
. The
bar involution
on
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{h}
is the
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
-linear map determined by

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\varnothing,\qquad\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v}\text{ for $i\in\{0,\dots,n\}$}.

The canonical basis for
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{h}
is written

\left\{\left.\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)\ \right|\ \smash{\beta\text{ a restricted $h$-strict partition}}\right\},

and the canonical basis vectors
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)
are defined by the following properties.

\diamond

\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)
is bar-invariant.

\diamond

\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)
has the form
\sum_{\alpha}\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}\alpha
, where
\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\beta\beta}=1
and
\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}
is a polynomial divisible by
q
for
\alpha\neq\beta
.

\diamond

\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}=0
unless
\alpha\trianglerighteqslant\beta
and
\alpha
has the same
h
-bar-core as
\beta
.

Our main focus in this paper is comparing canonical bases in types
A^{(1)}
and
A^{(2)}
.

3
Symmetric functions

In this section we recall some basic theory of symmetric functions, and prove an apparently new result which we will use later.

3.1
Background on symmetric functions

We take an indeterminate
t
over
\mathbb{Q}
, and a countably infinite set
X
of commuting indeterminates. We let
\Lambda
be the ring of
symmetric functions
: power series in the elements of
X
with bounded degree with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(t)
, which are invariant under permutations of
X
.

\Lambda
has several important bases. Two of the most important are the bases of
Schur functions

s_{\lambda}
and
Hall–Littlewood functions

P_{\lambda}
. We refer to Macdonald’s book
[
Mac
]
for definitions of these, as well as a detailed introduction to symmetric functions.

We will need to use the standard coproduct
\Delta:\Lambda\to\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
. To define this, first note that for any countably infinite set
Y
and any
f\in\Lambda
we can define
f(Y)
simply by replacing the elements of
X
with their images under some chosen bijection from
X
to
Y
. If we partition the set of variables
X
into two disjoint infinite sets
Y\sqcup Z
, then each symmetric function
f
is symmetric in the elements of
Y
and the elements of
Z
, so we can write
f=\sum_{i\in I}g_{i}(Y)h_{i}(Z)
for some finite indexing set
I
and
f_{i},g_{i}\in\Lambda
; we then define
\Delta(f)=\sum_{i}g_{i}\otimes h_{i}
.

Our aim is to study Pieri-type rules for Hall–Littlewood functions, and for this we need some notation. Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}
. Recall that we write
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
if
\lambda\subseteq\mu
and
\mu\setminus\lambda
consists of
r
nodes lying in different columns. (In the literature on symmetric functions, this is often expressed as saying that
\mu\setminus\lambda
is a
horizontal strip
of length
r
.) If this is the case, then we define

\operatorname{hs}_{\lambda\mu}(t)=\sum_{c}(1-t^{\mu^{\prime}_{c}-\mu^{\prime}_{c+1}}),

summing over all
c\geqslant 1
such that column
c
contains a node of
\mu\setminus\lambda
but column
c+1
does not. The polynomials
\operatorname{hs}_{\lambda\mu}(t)
are used to write down a “Pieri rule” for Hall–Littlewood functions. To define this, we need the second type of Hall–Littlewood functions
Q_{\lambda}
introduced by Macdonald. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
define

\displaystyle\phi_{n}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-t^{i}).

Now for any partition
\lambda
define

\displaystyle b_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\prod_{c}\phi_{\lambda^{\prime}_{c}-\lambda^{\prime}_{c+1}}.

Then
Q_{\lambda}
is defined by

\displaystyle Q_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=b_{\lambda}P_{\lambda}.

Now we can state Macdonald’s Pieri rule for Hall–Littlewood functions.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
Mac
, III.5.7]
)
.

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}
and
r\geqslant 0
then

P_{\lambda}Q_{(r)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathcal{P}\\
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu\end{subarray}}\operatorname{hs}_{\lambda\mu}(t)P_{\mu}.

3.2
A new Pieri rule

Our objective in this section is to prove a kind of dual rule to
3.1
, which surprisingly seems not to be in the literature.

Suppose
f\in\Lambda
. Because the Schur functions
s_{\lambda}
form a basis for
\Lambda
we can uniquely write
\Delta(f)=\sum_{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}(f)\otimes s_{\lambda}
for some symmetric functions
\partial_{\lambda}(f)
. This defines a function
\partial_{\lambda}:\Lambda\to\Lambda
for each partition
\lambda
. We are particularly interested in the functions
\partial_{(r)}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
. One form of the classical Pieri rule is that
\partial_{(r)}s_{\mu}=\sum s_{\lambda}
, summing over all
\lambda
such that
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
. To give the corresponding rule for
\partial_{(r)}P_{\mu}
, we need some more notation. If
\lambda,\mu
are partitions with
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
, define

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\lambda\mu}=\sum_{c}(1-t^{\lambda^{\prime}_{c}-\lambda^{\prime}_{c+1}}),

summing over all
c\geqslant 1
such that column
c+1
contains a node of
\lambda\setminus\mu
but column
c
does not.

The main result of this section is the following, which generalises part of
[
F3
, Proposition 3.6]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\partial_{(r)}P_{\mu}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{P}\\
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu\end{subarray}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\lambda\mu}P_{\lambda}.

We will show how to derive
3.2
from
3.1
using the self-duality of
\Lambda
explained by Konvalinka and Lauve in
[
KoL
, §2]
. The
Hall inner product
on
\Lambda
is the bilinear function defined by
\langle P_{\mu},Q_{\lambda}\rangle=\delta_{\mu\lambda}
. By
[
KoL
, Lemma 11]
,
\Lambda
is self-dual with respect to
\langle\,,\,\rangle
, which means that (defining
\langle\,,\,\rangle
on
\Lambda\otimes\Lambda
as well in the obvious way)

\langle\Delta(f),g\otimes h\rangle=\langle f,gh\rangle

for any
f,g,h\in\Lambda
.

The final ingredient we need is the following
Lemma
, which follows immediately from the definitions.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}
and
\lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu
. Then

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\lambda\mu}=\frac{b_{\lambda}\operatorname{hs}_{\lambda\mu}(t)}{b_{\mu}}.

To complete the proof of
3.2
, we need to consider the transition matrix between the bases
(s_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}
and
(P_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}
. This matrix is denoted
K(t)
, and its entries
K_{\lambda\mu}(t)
are called
Kostka polynomials
. Specifically, we write

s_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu}K_{\lambda\mu}(t)P_{\mu},\qquad P_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu}K^{-1}_{\lambda\mu}(t)s_{\mu}.

We will need the following properties of these polynomials.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}
. Then
K_{\lambda\mu}(t)
and
K^{-1}_{\lambda\mu}(t)
are polynomials in
t
which are zero unless
\lambda\trianglerighteqslant\mu
. Furthermore,
K_{\lambda\lambda}(t)=K^{-1}_{\lambda\lambda}(t)=1
, while
K_{\lambda\mu}(t)
and
K^{-1}_{\lambda\mu}(t)
are divisible by
t
when
\lambda\neq\mu
.

The given properties for
K_{\lambda\mu}(t)
follow from
[
Mac
, III.2.3 & III.2.6]
. Now the properties for
K^{-1}_{\lambda\mu}(t)
follow by inverting the matrix
K(t)
.
∎

Let
\left\{\left.S_{\lambda}\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}\right\}
be the basis dual to the basis of Schur functions with respect to
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
. Because
\left\{\left.Q_{\lambda}\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}\right\}
and
\left\{\left.s_{\lambda}\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}\right\}
are both bases for
\Lambda
, there are coefficients
a_{\mu\pi\nu}
defined for all
\mu,\pi,\nu\in\mathcal{P}
such that

\Delta(P_{\mu})=\sum_{\pi,\nu\in\mathcal{P}}a_{\mu\pi\nu}Q_{\pi}\otimes s_{\nu}.

Then

a_{\mu\pi\nu}=\langle\Delta(P_{\mu}),P_{\pi}\otimes S_{\nu}\rangle.

By
Lemma

3.4
we can write
s_{\lambda}
as
P_{\lambda}
plus a linear combination of the
P_{\mu}
with
\lambda\vartriangleright\mu
, for each
\lambda
. As a consequence,
S_{(r)}
equals
Q_{(r)}
. So

\displaystyle\partial_{(r)}P_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}}a_{\mu\pi(r)}Q_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}}\langle\Delta(P_{\mu}),P_{\pi}\otimes Q_{(r)}\rangle Q_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}}\langle P_{\mu},P_{\pi}Q_{(r)}\rangle b_{\pi}P_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\lambda\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\lambda\end{subarray}}\frac{\operatorname{hs}_{\pi\lambda}(t)}{b_{\mu}}\langle Q_{\mu},P_{\lambda}\rangle b_{\pi}P_{\pi}

by
3.1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu\end{subarray}}\frac{\operatorname{hs}_{\pi\mu}(t)b_{\pi}}{b_{\mu}}P_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\mu\end{subarray}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\mu}P_{\pi}.

by
Lemma

3.3
.∎

4
Comparing canonical bases in types
A^{(1)}_{n-1}
and
A^{(2)}_{h-1}

For the rest of the paper we fix a natural number
l
. We fix an odd integer
h\geqslant 3
, and let
n=\frac{1}{2}(h-1)
and
m=\frac{1}{2}(h+1)
.

Our aim in this paper is to compare the canonical bases in
\mathcal{V}_{m}
and
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{h}
. But as an intermediate step in this section, we compare
\mathcal{V}_{n}
with
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{h}
. So
for this section, we assume that
h\geqslant 5
.

We begin by setting up some combinatorics underlying our comparison between canonical bases. Say that an
h
-strict partition
\alpha
is
standard
if the residue modulo
h
of every non-zero part of
\alpha
lies in
\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
denote the set of standard
h
-strict partitions of length
l
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
denote the set of all partitions of length at most
l
. Proving the next
Section
is a routine exercise.

Proposition 4.1
.

Fix
l\geqslant 1
. There is a bijection
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
given by

\displaystyle(\phi\alpha)_{r}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{r}-(n+1)\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{r}}{h}\right\rfloor-l+r-1

for
1\leqslant r\leqslant l
. The inverse of
\phi
is given by

\displaystyle(\phi^{-1}\lambda)_{r}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{r}+l-r+1+(n+1)\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{r}+l-r}{n}\right\rfloor.

If
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, then
\beta
is restricted if and only if
\phi\beta
is
n
-restricted.

The bijection
\phi
is easily realised on the abacus. Given
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, the bar-abacus display for
\alpha
has
l
beads on runners
1,\dots,n
, no beads on runners
n+1,\dots,h-1
, and no beads on runner
0
except in position
0
. Deleting runners
0
and
n+1,\dots,h-1
yields the abacus display for
\phi\alpha
.

For example, taking
h=5
and
\alpha=(16,12,11,6,2)
, we obtain
\phi\alpha=(2^{3},1^{2})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 17.48 -59.43 L 17.48 -44.58}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.73 -52 L 19.23 -52}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\[22.0pt]
\alpha&\phi\alpha\end{array}

We want to compare canonical basis elements labelled by partitions in
\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
. So let

\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}=\left\langle\left.\lambda\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}}\right\rangle,\qquad\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}=\left\langle\left.\alpha\ \right|\ \smash{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}}\right\rangle,

where
\langle X\rangle
denotes the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-span of a set
X
. Now we have a bijective linear map
\Phi:\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}\to\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}
defined by mapping
\alpha\mapsto\phi\alpha
and extending linearly.

Observe that if
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
is
n
-restricted, then
\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu)\in\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}
, because if
\lambda
appears in
\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu)
with non-zero coefficient then
\lambda\trianglerighteqslant\mu
, so that
l(\lambda)\leqslant l
. A similar statement is true in
\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}
, but requires the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
. If
\alpha
is an
h
-strict partition with the same
h
-bar-core as
\beta
and
\alpha\trianglerighteqslant\beta
, then
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
.

First we observe that if we remove an
h
-bar from
\beta
, the resulting
h
-strict partition will be standard with length
l
: since
\beta
is standard, it does not have two parts which sum to
h
, and so removing an
h
-bar from
\beta
entails reducing by
h
a part which is larger than
h
. This does not change the length of the partition, or the set of residues modulo
h
of the non-zero parts. So the resulting partition is standard with length
l
. Applying this repeatedly, we find that the
h
-bar-core
\gamma
of
\beta
is standard with length
l
.

Now consider
\alpha
. Because
\alpha\trianglerighteqslant\beta
, the length of
\alpha
is at most
l
. On the other hand, by assumption the
h
-bar-core of
\alpha
is
\gamma
which has length
l
, and therefore
l(\alpha)=l
. We can obtain
\alpha
from
\gamma
by repeatedly adding
h
-bars. At each stage we do not increase the length of the partition, so addition of an
h
-bar must consist of increasing some positive part by
h
. This does not affect the set of residues modulo
h
of the non-zero parts, so
\alpha
is standard.
∎

As a consequence, if
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
is restricted, then
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)\in\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}
. Our first main result is the following.

Theorem 4.3
.

Suppose
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
is restricted. Then

\mathrm{G}_{n}(\phi\beta)=\Phi(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)).

Example
.

Take
h=5
, so that
n=2
. Let
\beta=(16,12,11,7,6,1)
. Then
\phi\beta=(1^{5})
. Writing
\mathrm{f}_{i}=\mathrm{f}_{i+2\mathbb{Z}}
for
i=0,1
, we can calculate

\big(\mathrm{f}_{0}\mathrm{f}_{1}\mathrm{f}_{0}\mathrm{f}_{1}\mathrm{f}_{0}-\mathrm{f}_{0}^{(2)}\mathrm{f}_{1}^{(2)}\mathrm{f}_{0}\big)\varnothing=(1^{5})+q^{2}(3,1^{2})+q^{4}(5),

so that

\mathrm{G}_{2}(1^{5})=(1^{5})+q^{2}(3,1^{2})+q^{4}(5).

On the other hand,
(12,11,7,6,2,1)
is a
5
-bar-core, so
(12,11,7,6,2,1)=\check{\mathrm{G}}_{5}(12,11,7,6,2,1)
is bar-invariant. If we write
\check{g}=\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2)}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{2}
and
\check{g}^{(2)}=\check{g}^{2}/(q^{2}+q^{-2})
, then

\big(\check{g}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}\check{g}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}\check{g}-\check{g}^{(2)}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}^{(2)}\check{g})(12,11,7,6,2,1)=(16,12,11,7,6,1)+q^{2}(21,12,11,6,2,1)+q^{4}(26,11,7,6,2,1)

is also bar-invariant, so equals
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{5}(\beta)
. So
\Phi(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{5}(\beta))=\mathrm{G}_{2}(\phi\beta)
.

The above example suggests how
4.3
is proved: we define linear operators
g_{0},\dots,g_{n-1}
on
\check{\mathcal{F}}
which correspond to the action of the generators
\mathrm{f}_{i}
on
\mathcal{F}
.

For the next two results we take
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, and let
\hat{\imath}=i-l+n\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
1\leqslant r\leqslant l
. Then
\alpha
has an
i
-bar-addable node
\mathfrak{b}
in row
r
if and only if
\phi\alpha
has an
\hat{\imath}
-addable node
\mathfrak{c}
in row
r
. If these nodes exist, then
\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b}
lies in
\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, and
\phi(\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b})=\phi\alpha\cup\mathfrak{c}
.

Since
\alpha
is standard,
\alpha_{r}
cannot be congruent to
h-i-1
modulo
h
, so
\alpha
has an
\hat{\imath}
-bar-addable node
\mathfrak{b}
in row
r
if and only if
\alpha_{r}\equiv i\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
and either
r=1
or
\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r}\geqslant 2
. Applying
\phi
, we see that this is equivalent to the condition that
\phi\alpha
has an
\hat{\imath}
-addable node
\mathfrak{c}
in row
r
.

If the nodes
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{c}
do exist, then
(\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b})_{r}=\alpha_{r}+1\equiv\hat{\imath}+1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
, so that
\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b}
is standard. Furthermore,
\phi(\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b})_{r}=(\phi\alpha)_{r}+1
, so
\phi(\alpha\cup\mathfrak{b})=\phi\alpha\cup\mathfrak{c}
.
∎

A corresponding result for
\hat{\imath}
-bar-removable nodes can be proved in exactly the same way. As a consequence, we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.5
.

If
\alpha
is a standard
h
-strict partition, then
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}\alpha\in\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}
, and

\Phi(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}\alpha)=\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha.

Since
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, adding an
i
-bar-addable node to a standard
h
-strict partition yields a standard
h
-strict partition; so the first statement holds.

Write
\lambda=\phi\alpha
. Then
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\lambda
is a linear combination of basis elements
\mu
obtained by adding a
\hat{\imath}
-addable node to
\lambda
. Take such a
\mu
, and suppose the added node lies in row
r
. Then
r\leqslant l
, since the residue of the addable node of
\lambda
in row
l+1
(if there is one) is
-l+n\mathbb{Z}\neq\hat{\imath}
. So
\mu=\phi\beta
, where
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
is obtained from
\alpha
by adding a node in row
r
. The coefficient of
\mu
in
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\lambda
is determined by the
\hat{\imath}
-addable and
\hat{\imath}
-removable nodes of
\lambda
below row
r
. These nodes all lie in row
l
or higher, so by
Lemma

4.4
(and its analogue for removable nodes) they correspond to the
i
-bar-addable and
i
-bar-removable nodes of
\alpha
in rows
r+1,\dots,l
. Furthermore,
\alpha
does not have an
i
-bar-addable node below row
l
(because
i\neq 0
). So (comparing the action of
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}
on
\mathcal{F}
with the action of
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}
on
\check{\mathcal{F}}
) the coefficients
(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}\alpha,\beta)
and
\left(\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\lambda,\mu\right)
agree.
∎

Now we consider the remaining residue not included in the last two results: let
\hat{\imath}=-l+n\mathbb{Z}
, and let
\check{g}=\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2)}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}\dots\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, and
\beta
is an
h
-strict partition with length
l
. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\left(\check{g}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
.

(2)

\beta
is standard and
\left(\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha,\phi\beta\right)\neq 0
.

Furthermore, if these two conditions hold, then
\left(\check{g}\alpha,\beta\right)=\left(\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha,\phi\beta\right)
.

Suppose
\beta
appears with non-zero coefficient in
\check{g}\alpha
. Then there is a sequence of
h
-strict partitions
\alpha(1),\dots,\alpha(n)
such that

\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\alpha(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n-1}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\alpha(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0:2}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\beta.

In particular,
\beta
has two nodes of bar-residue
0
that are not contained in
\alpha
. Let
r
be a row containing at least one of these nodes; then the node equals
(r,hc)
or
(r,hc+1)
for some
c\geqslant 1
. This means that
\alpha_{r}\leqslant hc
, but because
\alpha
is standard, this actually gives
\alpha_{r}\leqslant hc-n-1
, so that the nodes

(r,hc-n),\dots,(r,hc)

all belong to
\beta
but not to
\alpha
. The same applies for the other node of bar-residue
0
that lies in
\beta
but not in
\alpha
, so in fact these nodes must lie in the same row. Hence there is some
r\leqslant l
such that
\alpha_{r}\equiv n\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
and

\beta_{s}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{s}+n+2&\text{if }s=r\\
\alpha_{s}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular,
\beta
is standard. Furthermore, applying the definition of
\phi
we see that
\phi\beta=\phi\alpha\cup\mathfrak{n}
, where
\mathfrak{n}
is an
\hat{\imath}
-addable node of
\phi\alpha
. So (1)
\Rightarrow
(2), and the converse is similar (but easier).

It remains to compare the coefficient of
\beta
in
\check{g}\alpha
with the coefficient of
\phi\beta
in
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha
. To compute the coefficient of
\beta
in
\check{g}\alpha
, we consider bar-addable and bar-removable nodes below row
r
. The coefficient
\left(\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\lambda,\phi\beta\right)
includes a factor
q^{2}
for each bar-addable node of
\alpha
below row
r
, and a factor
q^{-2}
for each bar-removable node, except for
n
-bar-addable nodes which give a factor of
q^{4}
(note that
\alpha
has no
n
-bar-removable nodes because it is standard). The fact that
\alpha
is standard also means that for each
s>r
, the number of bar-removable nodes in row
s
equals the number of bar-addable nodes in row
s+1
, except when
\alpha_{s}\equiv 1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
, in which case there is one more bar-removable node in row
s
. Furthermore, there is an
n
-bar-addable node in row
s
if and only if
\alpha_{s}\equiv n\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
. So if we let
a_{1}
be the number of
s>r
such that
\alpha_{s}\equiv 1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
, and define
a_{n}
similarly, then

\left(\check{g}\alpha,\beta\right)=q^{2(a_{n}-a_{1}+1)}.

(The extra
1
arises from the bar-addable node in row
r+1
.)

But now observe that
\alpha_{s}\equiv n\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
if and only if
(\phi\alpha)_{s}-s\in\hat{\imath}-1
, while
\alpha_{s}\equiv 1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h)
if and only if
(\phi\alpha)_{s}-s\in\hat{\imath}
. So
a_{n}-a_{1}+1
equals the number of
i
-addable nodes of
\phi\lambda
below row
r
minus the number of
\hat{\imath}
-removable nodes (the extra
1
arises from the addable node in row
l+1
). So
\left(\check{g}\lambda,\beta\right)=\left(\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha,\phi\beta\right)
.
∎

This gives an analogue of
4.5
. We continue to write
\hat{\imath}=-l+n\mathbb{Z}
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose
\alpha
is a standard
h
-strict partition, and that
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha\in\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}
. Then
\check{g}\alpha\in\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}
, and

\Phi(\check{g}\alpha)=\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha.

From the fact that (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) in
4.6
we can write

\check{g}\alpha=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}}c_{\beta}\beta

for some coefficients
c_{\beta}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
, so that
\check{g}\alpha\in\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}
. Then the last statement in
4.6
tells us that for each
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
the coefficient of
\mu
in
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha
is
c_{\phi^{-1}\mu}
. By assumption no
\mu
with
\mu\notin\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
occurs in
\mathrm{f}_{\hat{\imath}}\phi\alpha
, and so

\mathrm{f}_{i}\phi\alpha=\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}}c_{\phi^{-1}\mu}\mu=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}}c_{\beta}\Phi(\beta)=\Phi(\check{g}\alpha).\qed

Now we can prove our first main result.

Take a restricted partition
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, and let
\mu=\phi\beta
. The LLT algorithm
[
LLT
, Section 6.2]
shows that
\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu)
can be written as a linear combination
\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}A(\nu)
, where:

(1)

the sum is over a set of partitions
\nu
with
\nu\trianglerighteqslant\mu
;

(2)

each coefficient
a_{\nu}
lies in
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
;

(3)

each
A(\nu)
has the form
\mathrm{f}_{i_{1}}\dots\mathrm{f}_{i_{r}}\varnothing
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
;

(4)

each
A(\nu)
is a linear combination of partitions
\xi
with
\xi\trianglerighteqslant\nu
.

In particular, conditions (
1
) and (
4
) imply that each
A(\nu)
lies in
\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}
. So if we take such a
\nu
and write

A(\nu)=\mathrm{f}_{i_{1}}\dots\mathrm{f}_{i_{r}}\varnothing=\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}}c_{\nu\xi}\xi

with each
c_{\nu\xi}\in\mathbb{C}(q)
, then by
4.5
and
4.7
we can find
j_{1},\dots,j_{s}\in\{0,\dots,n\}
and a coefficient
b_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
such that

\Phi^{-1}(A(\nu))=b_{\nu}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{j_{1}}\dots\check{\mathrm{f}}_{j_{s}}\phi^{-1}\varnothing=\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}}c_{\nu\xi}\phi^{-1}\xi.

(The coefficient
b_{\nu}
arises because of the divided power occurring in the definition of the operator
\check{g}
.) Hence the vector

\Phi^{-1}(\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu))=\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\Phi^{-1}(A(\nu))

can be written as a linear combination of vectors of the form
\check{\mathrm{f}}_{j_{1}}\dots\check{\mathrm{f}}_{j_{s}}\phi^{-1}\varnothing
, with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
. Now
\phi^{-1}\varnothing
is an
h
-bar-core: its parts are simply the smallest
l
positive integers whose residues modulo
h
lie in
\{1,\dots,n\}
. So
\phi^{-1}\varnothing=\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\phi^{-1}\varnothing)
is bar-invariant, and so
\Phi^{-1}(\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu))
is bar-invariant. Since
\Phi^{-1}(\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu))=\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}\phi^{-1}\lambda
, with
\operatorname{d}_{\mu\mu}=1
and all other
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
divisible by
q
, the uniqueness of the canonical basis means that
\Phi^{-1}(\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu))=\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)
.
∎

5
Comparing types
A^{(1)}_{n-1}
and
A^{(1)}_{m-1}
: runner addition

In this section we invoke a theorem from the author’s paper
[
F1
]
comparing canonical bases in types
A^{(1)}_{n-1}
and
A^{(1)}_{m-1}
, and use it to express
4.3
in terms of the canonical basis for
\mathcal{V}_{m}
; this will also allow us to include the case
h=3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
denote the set of partitions
\lambda
with length at most
l
such that
\lambda_{r}+l+1-r\not\equiv 0\ (\operatorname{mod}\,l)
for
1\leqslant r\leqslant l
. Another way of saying this is that
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
if
\lambda
can be displayed on an
m
-runner abacus with
l+1
beads such that runner
0
contains only a bead in position
0
. We define a bijection
\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}\to\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
which we denote
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{+}
, by setting

\lambda^{+}_{r}=\lambda_{r}+\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{r}+l-r}{n}\right\rfloor

for
r=1,\dots,l
. This bijection is easily visualised on the abacus: given
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
, we take the
n
-runner abacus display for
\lambda
with
l
beads, add a runner at the left with a bead in the top position only, and let
\lambda^{+}
be the resulting partition. It is easy to see that
\lambda^{+}
is
m
-restricted if and only if
\lambda
is
n
-restricted. Extending the map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{+}
linearly, we obtain an injective linear map
\mathcal{F}^{\leqslant l}\to\mathcal{F}
written
v\mapsto v^{+}
.

Now we can give the main result from
[
F1
]
. Some translation of notation is required, since in
[
F1
]
the opposite convention for
\mathcal{F}
is used, in which the canonical basis elements
\mathrm{G}_{n}(\mu)
are indexed by
n
-regular partitions, and a “full” runner is added rather than an empty one. But replacing all partitions with their conjugates yields the following result.

Theorem 5.1

(
[
F1
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Suppose
n\geqslant 2
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
is
n
-restricted. Then
\mathrm{G}_{m}(\lambda^{+})=\mathrm{G}_{n}(\lambda)^{+}
.

We combine this with the results of the last section to give a result comparing canonical bases in types
A^{(1)}_{m-1}
and
A^{(2)}_{h-1}
. Recalling the bijection
\phi:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
from
Section

4
, let
\psi:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}\to\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
be the bijection defined by
\psi\lambda=(\phi\lambda)^{+}
. Explicitly,
\psi
is given by

(\psi\alpha)_{r}=\alpha_{r}-(m-1)\left\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{r}}{h}\right\rfloor-l+r-1

for
1\leqslant r\leqslant l
.

We define an injective
\mathbb{C}(q)
-linear map
\Psi:\check{\mathcal{F}}^{l}\to\mathcal{F}
by
\Psi(\beta)=\psi\beta
. Now we obtain the following result.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
is restricted. Then

\mathrm{G}_{m}(\psi\beta)=\Psi(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\beta)).

If
h\geqslant 5
then this follows immediately from
4.3
and
5.1
. In the case
h=3
, the assumption that
\beta
is restricted means that
\beta
is the partition
(3l-2,3l-5,\dots,1)
, which is a
3
-bar-core, so that
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{3}(\beta)=\beta
. On the other hand,
\psi\beta
is the partition
(l,l-1,\dots,1)
which is a
2
-core, so that
\mathrm{G}_{2}(\psi\beta)=\psi\beta
. So the result follows in this case too.
∎

6
Comparing types
A^{(1)}_{m-1}
and
A^{(2)}_{h-1}
: separated partitions

Having established
5.2
, we will work with
U_{m}
rather than
U_{n}
from now on. With this in mind, if
a
is an integer then we may write
\mathrm{f}_{a}
to mean
\mathrm{f}_{a+m\mathbb{Z}}
.

Our next aim is to extend
5.2
to include
h
-strict partitions with positive parts divisible by
h
. To do this, we need to restrict attention to what we call
separated
partitions. In this section we define separated partitions and prove two preparatory results giving similar calculations in
\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\mathcal{F}}
.

6.1
Separated partitions

We keep
l\in\mathbb{N}
fixed. Given a partition
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and any partition
\pi
, we define a partition
\pi|\lambda
as follows: we take the abacus display for
\lambda
with
m
runners and
am+l+1
beads for sufficiently large
a
, and then move the
r
th lowest bead on runner
0
down
\pi_{r}
positions for each
r
. Another way to express this is as follows: we write
\lambda=\mu^{+}
for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\leqslant l}
; then we join together the
1
-runner abacus display for
\pi
with
a+1
beads and
n
-runner abacus display for
\mu
with
an+l
beads. It is easy to see that
\pi|\lambda
is independent of the choice of
a
.

Given such an abacus display for
\pi|\lambda
, let
b
be the position of the last bead on runner
0
(that is,
b=(\pi_{1}+a)m
), and let
f
be the first empty position not on runner
0
. Given
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we say that
\pi|\lambda
is
k
-separated
if
f-b>km
. For
k=0
, we will just say
separated
rather than
0
-separated. Whether
\pi|\lambda
is
k
-separated is independent of the choice of
a
(in fact, it only depends on
l
,
k
,
\pi_{1}
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{1}
).

Example
.

Take
h=7
, so that
m=4
, and let
l=10
. If we take
\lambda=(7,6^{2},4,1^{3})
and
\pi=(1^{2})
, then
\pi|\lambda=(7,6^{2},4,1^{11})
, as we see from the following abacus displays.
