\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\[38.0pt]
\lambda&\pi|\lambda\end{array}

We can see that
\pi|\lambda
is
1
-separated but not
2
-separated.

Now for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we define an operator
F_{k}
on the Fock space by

F_{k}=\mathrm{f}_{-l}^{(k)}\mathrm{f}_{1-l}^{(k)}\dots\mathrm{f}_{m-1-l}^{(k)}.

The first result we want to prove describes
F_{k}(\pi|\lambda)
when
\pi|\lambda
is
k
-separated. Recall that given two partitions
\pi,\rho
, we write
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
if
\rho
can be obtained from
\pi
by adding
r
nodes in distinct columns.

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
,
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
and
k,u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and that
\pi|\lambda
is
(k+u)
-separated. If
\nu\in\mathcal{P}
, then
\left(F_{k}(\pi|\lambda),\nu\right)\neq 0
if and only if
\nu
has the form
\rho|\mu
, where

\diamond

\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
,

\diamond

\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
for some
0\leqslant r\leqslant k
, and

\diamond

\left(F_{k-r}\lambda,\mu\right)\neq 0
.

Furthermore, if these conditions hold, then
\rho|\mu
is
u
-separated.

In this proof we use an abacus with
m
runners and
l+am+1
beads for fixed large
a
. For any partition
\nu
we write
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
for the
m
-runner abacus display for
\nu
with
l+am+1
beads.
Given a finite set
B
of positions on the abacus, we use the phrase
making bead moves from
B
to mean moving a bead from position
b
to position
b+1
for each
b\in B
.

Suppose
\nu
appears in
F_{k}(\pi|\lambda)
with non-zero coefficient. From the description of the action of the
\mathrm{f}_{i}
on
\mathcal{F}_{m}
(interpreted in terms of the abacus) there are sets
B_{0},\dots,B_{m-1}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
\lvert B_{g}\rvert=k
and
B_{g}\subset g+m\mathbb{Z}
for each
g
, such that
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
is obtained from
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
by making bead moves from
B_{m-1},B_{m-2},\dots,B_{0}
in turn. We write
B=B_{0}\cup\dots\cup B_{m-1}
.

Let
xm
be the position of the last bead on runner
0
in
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
; that is,
x=\pi_{1}+a
. The assumption that
\pi|\lambda
is
(k+u)
-separated means that every position before position
(x+k+u)m
not on runner
0
is occupied in
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
. We make two observations about the relationships between the sets
B_{0},\dots,B_{m-1}
.

(i)

If
b\in B_{0}
with
b>xm
, then
b-1\in B_{m-1}
(otherwise it is not possible to make a bead move from
b
at the final step).

(ii)

If
b\in B_{g}
with
0\leqslant g<m-1
and
b<(x+k+u)m
, then
b+1\in B_{g+1}
(so that there is a space to move a bead into from position
b
when making bead moves from
B_{g}
). Applying this repeatedly, we deduce that if
b\in B_{0}
with
b<(x+k+u)m
, then
b+m-1\in B_{m-1}
.

Since
\lvert B_{0}\rvert=k
, we can find
y\in\{x,x+1,\dots,x+k\}
such that
ym\notin B_{0}
. Now the two observations above imply that there is a function

\displaystyle f:B_{0}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B_{m-1}

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}b+m-1&\text{if }b<ym\\
b-1&\text{if }b>ym\end{cases}

which is injective, and hence bijective. Now we partition each
B_{g}
as
C_{g}\cup D_{g}
, where

C_{g}=\left\{\left.b\in B_{g}\ \right|\ \smash{b<ym}\right\},\qquad D_{g}=\left\{\left.b\in B_{g}\ \right|\ \smash{b>ym}\right\}.

The fact that
f
is bijective together with observation
(ii)
above means that

C_{g}=C_{0}+g\text{ for }0\leqslant g\leqslant m-1,

so that in particular
\lvert C_{0}\rvert=\dots=\lvert C_{m-1}\rvert=r
, say. The fact that
f
is bijective also gives

D_{m-1}+1=D_{0}.

So the combined bead moves from
D_{m-1},\dots,D_{0}
have no effect on runner
0
. So (
6.1
) implies that runner
0
of
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
is obtained from runner
0
of
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
by adding a bead at position
b+m
for all
b\in C_{0}
, and then removing a bead from position
b
for all
b\in C_{0}
. In particular, there are exactly
a
beads on runner
0
in
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
, and the last bead on runner
0
is in position
ym
or earlier. (
6.1
) also means that all the positions before position
ym
on runners other than runner
0
are occupied in
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
. This means that
\nu
has the form
\rho|\mu
with
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
. Now we compare the beta-sets
\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\rho)
. If we let
A=\left\{\left.c/m+1\ \right|\ \smash{c\in C_{0}}\right\}
, then the above description of runner
0
of
\operatorname{Ab}(\nu)
means that
\lvert A\rvert=r
,
A\cap\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)=\emptyset
, and
\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\rho)=\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)\cup A\setminus(A-1)
. So
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
by
Lemma

2.1
(2).

Another consequence of (
6.1
) is that the combined bead moves from
C_{m-1},\dots,C_{0}
have no effect on any runner other than runner
0
. This means that if we take
\operatorname{Ab}(\lambda)
and make the bead moves from
D_{m-1},\dots,D_{0}
in turn, then we obtain
\operatorname{Ab}(\mu)
. So
\mu
appears in
F_{k-r}\lambda
with non-zero coefficient.

Now we show that
\rho|\mu
is
u
-separated. Let
D=D_{0}\cup\dots\cup D_{m-1}
. Because
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
, the last bead on runner
0
in
\operatorname{Ab}(\rho|\mu)
is in position
(x+r)m
or earlier. Let
d
be the first empty position in
\operatorname{Ab}(\rho|\mu)
not on runner
0
. If
d>(x+k+u)m
, then certainly
\rho|\mu
is
u
-separated, so assume
d<(x+k+u)m
. Then position
d
is occupied in
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
, so
d\in D
. Now observation
(ii)
and (
6.1
) imply that
d+1,d+2,\dots,(x+k+u)m
all belong to
D
as well. Hence

m(k-r)=\lvert D\rvert>(x+k+u)m-d,

giving
d>(x+r+u)m
, so that
\rho|\mu
is
u
-separated.

Now we need to prove the “if” part of the
Section
. So suppose we are given
\mu,\rho,r
satisfying the conditions in the
Section
. Because
\mu
appears in
F_{k-r}\lambda
with non-zero coefficient,
\operatorname{Ab}(\mu)
is obtained from the abacus display for
\lambda
by making bead moves from
D_{m-1},\dots,D_{0}
in turn, where
D_{g}\subset g+m\mathbb{Z}
and
\lvert D_{g}\rvert=k-r
for each
g
. In addition,
D_{0}=D_{m-1}+1
because
\lambda
and
\mu
have identical configurations on the
0
th runner. In the same way as in the last paragraph, we can show that every element of
D_{0}\cup\dots\cup D_{m-1}
is greater than
(x+r+u)m
.

The assumption that
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
means (using
Lemma

2.1
(2)) that
\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\rho)=\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)\cup A\setminus(A-1)
for some set
A
with
\lvert A\rvert=r
and
A\cap\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)=\emptyset
. So if we define

C_{g}=\left\{\left.(a-1)m+g\ \right|\ \smash{a\in A}\right\}

for each
g
, then
\operatorname{Ab}(\rho|\mu)
is obtained from
\operatorname{Ab}(\pi|\lambda)
by making bead moves from
C_{m-1}\cup D_{m-1},\dots,C_{0}\cup D_{0}
in turn, so
\rho|\mu
appears in
F_{k}(\pi|\lambda)
.
∎

Now we want to show that the coefficients appearing in
6.1
coincide.

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and
\pi,\rho\in\mathcal{P}
with
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
for some
r
, and that
k\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
k\geqslant r
and
\pi|\lambda
is
k
-separated. Then

\left(F_{k}(\pi|\lambda),\rho|\mu\right)=\left(F_{k-r}\lambda,\mu\right)

6.1
shows that one side of the given equation is non-zero if and only if the other is, so we assume they are both non-zero. We use an abacus with
l+am+1
beads, and we define the integer
y
and the sets
B_{g}=C_{g}\cup D_{g}
for
0\leqslant g\leqslant m-1
as in the proof of
6.1
. Then
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by making bead moves from
D_{m-1},\dots,D_{0}
in turn, and
\rho|\mu
is obtained from
\pi|\lambda
by making bead moves from
B_{m-1},\dots,B_{0}
in turn. We remark that these sets are uniquely defined: if
\rho|\mu
appears in
F_{k}(\pi|\lambda)=\mathrm{f}_{-l}^{(k)}\mathrm{f}_{1-l}^{(k)}\dots\mathrm{f}_{-1-l}^{(k)}(\pi|\lambda)
, then we obtain
\rho|\mu
by adding all the
(-1-l)
-nodes of
(\rho|\mu)\setminus(\pi|\lambda)
, then all the
(-2-l)
-nodes of
(\rho|\mu)\setminus(\pi|\lambda)
, and so on, with no choice at any stage.

So if we define partitions
\lambda^{(0)},\dots,\lambda^{(m)}
by setting
\lambda^{(0)}=\lambda
and defining
\lambda^{(g)}
from
\lambda^{(g-1)}
by making bead moves from
D_{m-g}
for
g\geqslant 1
, then
\lambda^{(m)}=\mu
and

\left(F_{k-r}\lambda,\mu\right)=\prod_{g=1}^{m}\left(\mathrm{f}_{-g-l}^{(k-r)}\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)}\right),

and the formula for the action of
\mathrm{f}_{-g-l}^{(k-r)}
says that

\left(\mathrm{f}_{-g-l}^{(k-r)}\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)}\right)=q^{2n(\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)})}.

Similarly we define partitions
\xi^{(0)},\dots,\xi^{(m)}
by setting
\xi^{(0)}=\pi|\lambda
and then making bead moves from
B_{m-1},\dots,B_{0}
, and derive a similar formula for
\left(F_{k}(\pi|\lambda),\rho|\mu\right)
. We compare
n(\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)})
with
n(\xi^{(g-1)},\xi^{(g)})
for each
g
.

Taking
2\leqslant g\leqslant m-1
first and letting
i=-g-l+m\mathbb{Z}
, we compare the addable and removable
i
-nodes of
\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)},\xi^{(g-1)},\xi^{(g)}
, by looking at runners
m-g
and
m-g+1
of their abacus displays. For any given row we may give the configuration of beads on the two positions on runner
m-g
and
m-g+1
in that row.

The rows on these runners where these four partitions differ are the rows containing elements of
C_{m-g}
, where

\diamond

\lambda^{(g-1)}
and
\lambda^{(g)}
both have
,

\diamond

\xi^{(g-1)}
has
,

\diamond

\xi^{(g)}
has
,

and the rows containing elements of
D_{m-g}
, where

\diamond

\lambda^{(g-1)}
and
\xi^{(g-1)}
both have
,

\diamond

\lambda^{(g)}
and
\xi^{(g)}
both have
.

We conclude that
\lambda^{(g-1)}
and
\xi^{(g-1)}
have exactly the same removable
i
-nodes while
\lambda^{(g)}
and
\xi^{(g)}
have exactly the same addable
i
-nodes. Moreover, none of the addable
i
-nodes of
\xi^{(g)}
or removable
i
-nodes of
\xi^{(g-1)}
lie to the left of any of the added nodes corresponding to the positions in
C_{m-g}
. So
n(\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)})=n(\xi^{(g-1)},\xi^{(g)})
, which means that
\left(\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(k-r)}\lambda^{(g-1)},\lambda^{(g)}\right)=\left(\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(k)}\xi^{(g-1)},\xi^{(g)}\right)
.

Now we consider the case
g=1
, by comparing runners
m-1
and
0
of
\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)},\xi^{(0)},\xi^{(1)}
. To help in visualising addable and removable nodes, we imagine runner
m-1
moved to lie to the left of runner
0
, and shifted down so that position
cm-1
is directly to the left of position
cm
for each
c
. So for a given partition when we show the abacus configuration on these runners in row
c
, we show positions
cm-1
and
cm
. (We temporarily introduce a new position
-1
, which is taken to be occupied in all abacus displays.)

After position
ym
, the partitions
\lambda^{(0)}
and
\xi^{(0)}
agree, as do the partitions
\lambda^{(1)}
and
\xi^{(1)}
. Now we look at the rows before position
ym
.

The abacus displays for
\lambda^{(0)}
and
\lambda^{(1)}
both have
in the first
a+1
rows, and
in the remaining
y-a
rows up to position
ym
.

Now consider the positions
cm-1
and
cm
in the abacus displays for
\xi^{(0)}
and
\xi^{(1)}
, where
cm\leqslant y
. The configurations in these positions are determined by the sets
C_{m-1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)
:

\diamond

if
cm-1\in C_{m-1}
, then
\xi^{(0)}
has
and
\xi^{(1)}
has
;

\diamond

if
cm-1\notin C_{m-1}
and
c\notin\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)
, then
\xi^{(0)}
and
\xi^{(1)}
both have
;

\diamond

if
c\in\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi)
, then
\xi^{(0)}
and
\xi^{(1)}
both have
.

Note in particular that the second possibility happens exactly
y-a-r
times. This enables us to compare
n(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})
with
n(\xi^{(0)},\xi^{(1)})
by examining addable and removable nodes. We find that

n(\xi^{(0)},\xi^{(1)})=n(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})-(k-r)r+|A|,

where

A=\left\{\left.(c,d)\ \right|\ \smash{0\leqslant c<d,\ cm-1\notin C_{m-1},\ c\notin\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi),\ dm-1\in C_{m-1}}\right\}.

We perform a similar analysis for the case
g=m
, and obtain

n(\xi^{(m-1)},\xi^{(m)})=n(\lambda^{(m-1)},\lambda^{(m)})+(k-r)r-|B|,

where

B=\left\{\left.0\leqslant c<d\leqslant y\ \right|\ \smash{cm-1\notin C_{m-1},\ c\notin\mathcal{B}_{a+1}(\pi),\ dm\in C_{0}}\right\}.

Now recall from the proof of
6.1
that
C_{m-1}=C_{0}+m-1
. This then means that there is a bijection
A\to B
given by
(c,d)\mapsto(c,d-1)
. We deduce that

n(\xi^{(0)},\xi^{(1)})+n(\xi^{(m-1)},\xi^{(m)})=n(\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)})+n(\lambda^{(m-1)},\lambda^{(m)}),

and therefore

\left(\mathrm{f}_{-l}^{(k)}\xi^{(0)},\xi^{(1)}\right)\left(\mathrm{f}_{1-l}^{(k)}\xi^{(m-1)},\xi^{(m)}\right)=\left(\mathrm{f}_{-l}^{(k-r)}\lambda^{(0)},\lambda^{(1)}\right)\left(\mathrm{f}_{1-l}^{(k-r)}\lambda^{(m-1)},\lambda^{(m)}\right),

which is enough to complete the proof.
∎

6.2
Bar-separated
h
-strict partitions

Now we prove similar results for
h
-strict partitions. Keep
l
fixed, and suppose
\alpha
is a standard
h
-strict partition of length
l
. Given a partition
\pi
, define
\pi\|\alpha=\alpha\sqcup h\pi
. So the abacus display for
\pi\|\alpha
with
h
runners is obtained by taking the abacus display for
\alpha
and adding a bead at position
\pi_{r}h
for each
r
. Let
b
be the position of the last bead on runner
0
(so
b=\pi_{1}h
), and let
f
be the first empty position on any of runners
1,\dots,n
. Given
k\geqslant 0
, say that
\pi\|\alpha
is
k
-bar-separated

f-b>kh
. (We write simply “bar-separated” to mean “
0
-bar-separated”.)

Now for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\check{F}_{k}=\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2k)}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{1}^{(2k)}\dots\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-1}^{(2k)}\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k)}
.
Then we can give an analogue of
6.1
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
,
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
and
k,u\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and that
\pi\|\alpha
is
(k+u)
-bar-separated. If
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{(h)}
, then
\left(\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\gamma\right)\neq 0
if and only if
\gamma
has the form
\rho\|\beta
, where

\diamond

\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
,

\diamond

\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
for some
0\leqslant r\leqslant k
, and

\diamond

\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
.

Furthermore, if these conditions hold, then
\rho\|\beta
is
u
-bar-separated.

We follow the structure of the proof of
6.1
, though the details here are slightly different. For any
h
-strict partition
\gamma
, we write
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\gamma)
for the bar-abacus display of
\gamma
. As in the proof of
6.1
, given a finite set
B\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we use the phrase
making bead moves from
B
to mean moving a bead from position
b
to position
b+1
for each
b\in B
.

Suppose
\gamma
appears in
\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha)
. Let
\beta
be the partition obtained from
\gamma
by removing all the positive parts divisible by
h
, and let
\rho
be the partition obtained by taking all the parts of
\gamma
divisible by
h
and dividing them all by
h
. The assumption that
\left(\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\gamma\right)\neq 0
means that we can obtain
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\gamma)
from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
by making bead moves from

B_{n},B_{n+1}\cup B_{n-1},B_{n+2}\cup B_{n-2},\dots,B_{h-1}\cup B_{0}

in turn, where each
B_{g}
is a set of non-negative integers congruent to
g
modulo
h
, and
\lvert B_{n}\rvert=k
while
\lvert B_{n+g}\rvert+\lvert B_{n-g}\rvert=2k
for
1\leqslant g\leqslant n
.

Let
x=\pi_{1}
. Then the lowest bead on runner
0
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
is in position
xh
. Because
\lvert B_{n}\rvert=k
, we can choose
y\in\{x,\dots,x+k\}
such that
yh+n\notin B_{n}
. This has two consequences.

\diamond

Because
\pi\|\alpha
is
k
-bar-separated, positions
yh+1,\dots,yh+n
are occupied in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
. The assumption that
yh+n\notin B_{n}
then means that
yh+g\notin B_{g}
for
g=1,\dots,n-1
.

\diamond

Because
\alpha
is standard, runners
n+1,\dots,h-1
are empty in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
and position
(y+1)h
is unoccupied. The assumption that
yh+n\notin B_{n}
then means that
yh+g\notin B_{g}
for
g=n+1,\dots,h-1
, and
(y+1)h\notin B_{0}
.

Now write
B_{g}=C_{g}\cup D_{g}
for each
g
, where

C_{g}=\left\{\left.b\in B_{g}\ \right|\ \smash{b\leqslant yh}\right\},\qquad D_{g}=\left\{\left.b\in B_{g}\ \right|\ \smash{b>yh}\right\}.

The fact that runners
n+1,\dots,h-1
are empty in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
, and that there are no beads on runner
0
after position
yh
, means that
C_{n+g}\subseteq C_{n+g-1}+1
and
D_{n+g}\subseteq D_{n+g-1}+1
for
g=1,\dots,n
, and also that
D_{0}\subseteq D_{n}+n+1
. In addition, the fact that all the positions on runners
1,\dots,n
before position
yh
are occupied in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
means that
C_{n-g}\subseteq C_{n-g+1}-1
for
g=1,\dots,n
. So

\lvert C_{0}\rvert\leqslant\dots\leqslant\lvert C_{n}\rvert\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lvert C_{h-1}\rvert

and

\lvert D_{n}\rvert\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lvert D_{h-1}\rvert\geqslant\lvert D_{0}\rvert

Now the fact that
\lvert C_{h-1}\rvert+\lvert C_{0}\rvert+\lvert D_{h-1}\rvert+\lvert D_{0}\rvert=2k=2\lvert C_{n}\rvert+2\lvert D_{n}\rvert
gives equality everywhere. So
C_{g}=C_{0}+g
for
g=1,\dots,h-1
and
D_{n+g}=D_{n}+g
for
g=1,\dots,n
, and
D_{0}=D_{n}+n+1
.

So the combined bead moves from
C_{n},C_{n+1}\cup C_{n-1},\dots,C_{h-1}\cup C_{0}
only affect runner
0
in the bar-abacus display, where the effect is to add a bead at position
b+h
for each
b\in C_{0}
and then remove a bead from position
b
for each
b\in C_{0}
. By
Lemma

2.1
(1), this means that
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
.

Now look at the other runners in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\gamma)
. From what we have learned about the sets
C_{g}
and
D_{g}
, we know that there are
l
beads on runners
1,\dots,n
, and no beads on runners
n+1,\dots,h-1
. So
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, and
\gamma=\rho\|\beta
. Furthermore,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\beta)
is obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
by making bead moves from
D_{n},D_{n+1}\cup D_{n-1},\dots,D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
, and
\lvert D_{g}\rvert=k-r
for each
g
, so
\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
.

We also need to show that
\rho\|\beta
is
u
-bar-separated; this is done in the same way as the corresponding part of
6.1
.

Now we prove the “if” part of the
Section
. Suppose we are given
\beta,\rho,r
as in the
Section
. Because
\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
, there are sets
D_{0},\dots,D_{h-1}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
D_{g}\subset g+h\mathbb{Z}
for each
g
and
\lvert D_{n+g}\rvert+\lvert D_{n-g}\rvert=2(k-r)
for
g=0,\dots,n
, such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\beta)
is obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
by making bead moves from
D_{n},D_{n+1}\cup D_{n-1},\dots,D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
in turn. Because
\alpha
and
\beta
are both standard, we obtain
D_{n+g}=D_{n}+g
for
g=1,\dots,n
, so that in fact
\lvert D_{g}\rvert=k-r
for every
g
.

The assumption that
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
means that there is a set
A\subset\mathbb{N}
with
\lvert A\rvert=r
such that
\rho
is obtained from
\pi
by adding a part equal to
a
for every
a\in A
, and then removing a part equal to
a-1
for each
a\in A
. So if we define

C_{g}=\left\{\left.(a-1)h+g\ \right|\ \smash{a\in A}\right\},\qquad B_{g}=C_{g}\cup D_{g}

for each
g
, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\rho\|\beta)
is obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\pi\|\alpha)
by making bead moves from
B_{n},B_{n+1}\cup B_{n-1},B_{h-1}\cup B_{0}
in turn, so
\rho\|\beta
appears in
\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha)
.
∎

Now we compare the coefficients appearing in
6.3
. This is similar to
6.2
, though here the statement is more complicated. Given partitions
\pi,\rho
with
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
, we let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}
be defined as in
Section

3.2
,
with the indeterminate
t
replaced by
-q^{2}
; that is,

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\geqslant 1\\
\rho^{\prime}_{c}=\pi^{\prime}_{c}\\
\rho^{\prime}_{c+1}>\pi^{\prime}_{c+1}\end{subarray}}(1-(-q^{2})^{\pi^{\prime}_{c}-\pi^{\prime}_{c+1}}).

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
\pi,\rho\in\mathcal{P}
with
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
for some
r
, and that
\pi\|\alpha
is
k
-bar-separated for some
k\geqslant r
. Then

\left(\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\rho\|\beta\right)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right).

6.3
shows that one side of the given equation is non-zero if and only if the other is, so we assume they are both non-zero. We define the integer
y
and the sets
B_{g}=C_{g}\cup D_{g}
for
0\leqslant g\leqslant h-1
as in the proof of
6.3
. Then
\beta
is obtained from
\alpha
by making bead moves from
D_{n},D_{n+1}\cup D_{n-1},\dots,D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
in turn, and
\rho\|\beta
is obtained from
\pi\|\alpha
by making bead moves from
B_{n},B_{n+1}\cup B_{n-1},\dots,B_{h-1}\cup B_{0}
in turn. As in the proof of
6.2
, these sets are uniquely defined.

Define partitions
\alpha^{(0)},\dots,\alpha^{(m)}
by letting
\alpha^{(0)}=\alpha
, and then constructing
\alpha^{(g+1)}
from
\alpha^{(g)}
by making bead moves from
B_{n+g}\cup B_{n-g}
, for
g=0,\dots,n-1
. Then
\alpha^{(m)}=\beta
, and

\displaystyle\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k-r)}\alpha,\alpha^{(1)}\right)\ \mathrlap{\times\ \prod_{g=1}^{n}\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2(k-r))}\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)}\right).}

Correspondingly,

\displaystyle\left(F_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\rho\|\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k)}(\pi\|\alpha),\pi\|\alpha^{(1)}\right)\

\displaystyle\times\ \prod_{g=1}^{n-1}\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2k)}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)}),\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\times\ \left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2k)}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)}),\rho\|\beta\right).

First we compare the terms

\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k-r)}\alpha,\alpha^{(1)}\right)=q^{4\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})},\qquad\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k)}(\pi\|\alpha),\pi\|\alpha^{(1)}\right)=q^{4\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha,\pi\|\alpha^{(1)})}.

We can compute the integers
\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})
and
\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha,\pi\|\alpha^{(1)})
by looking at runners
n
and
n+1
in the bar-abacus. As in the proof of
6.2
, we may draw a small diagram showing the configuration of beads on these runners in a given row. These two runners are identical in the four partitions
\alpha,\alpha^{(1)},\pi\|\alpha,\pi\|\alpha^{(1)}
, except:

\diamond

in the positions corresponding to elements of
C_{n}
, where
\alpha
,
\alpha^{(1)}
and
\pi\|\alpha
all have
while
\pi\|\alpha^{(1)}
has
;

\diamond

in the positions corresponding to elements of
D_{n}
, where
\alpha
and
\pi\|\alpha
have
while
\alpha^{(1)}
and
\pi\|\alpha^{(1)}
have
.

Now comparing the calculation of
\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})
and
\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha,\pi\|\alpha^{(1)})
, we obtain

\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha,\pi\|\alpha^{(1)})=\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})-(k-r)r+\lvert A\rvert,

where

A=\left\{\left.0\leqslant c<d\ \right|\ \smash{ch+n\notin C_{n}\ni dh+n}\right\}.

Next we take
1\leqslant g\leqslant n-1
, and compare the terms

\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2(k-r))}\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)}\right)=q^{2\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})},\qquad\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2k)}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)}),\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)}\right)=q^{2\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)},\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)})}.

We can compare the integers
\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})
and
\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)},\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)})
by looking at runners
n-g,n-g+1,n+g,n+g+1
of the bar-abacus.

On runners
n-g
and
n-g+1
the four partitions have identical configurations, except:

\diamond

in the positions corresponding to elements of
C_{n-g}
, where
\alpha^{(g)}
and
\alpha^{(g+1)}
have
, while
\pi\|\alpha^{(g)}
has
and
\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)}
has
;

\diamond

in the positions corresponding to elements of
D_{n-g}
, where
\alpha^{(g)}
and
\pi\|\alpha^{(g)}
have
while
\alpha^{(g+1)}
and
\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)}
have
.

A similar statement holds for runners
n+g
and
n+g+1
, but with
in place of
. Now comparing the calculation of
\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})
and
\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)},\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)})
using the bar-abacus gives

\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})=\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(g)},\pi\|\alpha^{(g+1)}).

Finally we look at the terms

\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2(k-r))}\alpha^{(n)},\beta\right)=N_{1}q^{\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)},\qquad\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2k)}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)}),\rho\|\beta\right)=N_{2}q^{\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)}.

Here
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are products of terms
1-(-q^{2})^{b}
which we will deal with at the end of the proof.

To compare the calculation of
\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)
and
\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)
it is easier to use Young diagrams directly rather than the bar-abacus. Given a pair of columns
c<d
where
c,d
are each congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
h
, let’s write

\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)=\begin{cases}1&\parbox{205.0pt}{if there is a node of $\beta\setminus\alpha^{(n)}$ in column $d$ and a $0$-bar-addable node of $\beta$ in column $c$}\\[9.0pt]
-1&\parbox{205.0pt}{if there is a node of $\beta\setminus\alpha^{(n)}$ in column $d$ and a $0$-bar-removable node of $\alpha^{(n)}$ in column $c$}\\[9.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We define
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)
similarly. Then

\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)-\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)=\sum_{c<d}\left(\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)-\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)\right).

We can explicitly list the pairs
c<d
for which
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)\neq\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)
. These occur in two ways.

\diamond

If
d-1\in C_{h-1}\cup C_{0}
while
c-1\notin C_{h-1}\cup C_{0}
, then
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)=0
while
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)=-1
.

\diamond

If
d-1\in D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
and
c-1\in C_{h-1}\cup C_{0}
, then
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)=-1
while
\operatorname{ar}_{c,d}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)=0
.

Summing over all these pairs, we obtain

\displaystyle\check{n}(\pi\|\alpha^{(n)},\rho\|\beta)-\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)

\displaystyle=(\lvert C_{0}\rvert+\lvert C_{h-1}\rvert)(\lvert D_{0}\rvert+\lvert D_{h-1}\rvert)-\lvert B\rvert

\displaystyle=4r(k-r)-\lvert B\rvert,

where

B=\left\{\left.(c,d)\ \right|\ \smash{1\leqslant c<d,\ c\equiv 0,1\ (\operatorname{mod}\,h),\ c-1\notin(C_{0}\cup C_{h-1}),\ d-1\in(C_{0}\cup C_{h-1})}\right\}.

But now recalling that
C_{0}=C_{n}-n
and
C_{h-1}=C_{n}+n
gives

B=\left\{\left.(ch\pm n,dh\pm n)\ \right|\ \smash{(c,d)\in A}\right\},

where
A
is the set defined earlier in the proof. So
\lvert B\rvert=4\lvert A\rvert
.

Now combining all the cases we have computed to calculate
\left(F_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\rho\|\beta\right)/\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)
, the powers of
q
cancel to give

\frac{\left(F_{k}(\pi\|\alpha),\rho\|\beta\right)}{\left(\check{F}_{k-r}\alpha,\beta\right)}=\frac{N_{2}}{N_{1}},

and we just need to show that
N_{2}/N_{1}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}
. Recall that
N_{1}
is the product of terms
1-(-q^{2})^{b}
, where we take the product over all
c\geqslant 1
such that
\beta\setminus\alpha^{(n)}
includes a node in column
ch+1
but not column
c
, and
b
is the number of times
c
appears in
\alpha^{(n)}
. But in fact there are no such
c
, because the nodes of
\beta\setminus\alpha^{(n)}
correspond to elements of
D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
, and
D_{0}=D_{h-1}+1
. So
N_{1}=1
. So we need to consider
N_{2}
, which is defined in a similar way. The values of
c
for which
\rho\|\beta\setminus\pi\|\alpha^{(n)}
includes a node in column
ch+1
but not column
c
are the values such that
ch\in C_{0}
but
ch-1\notin C_{h-1}
. These in turn are precisely the values of
c
for which
\rho^{\prime}_{c+1}>\pi^{\prime}_{c+1}
while
\rho^{\prime}_{c}=\pi^{\prime}_{c}
. As a result,
N_{2}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}
, as required.
∎

7
Comparing canonical bases

In this section we give our main results comparing canonical bases. In order to use the results of
Sections

6.1
and
6.2
, we need to compare the actions of the operators
F_{k}
and
\check{F}_{k}
defined in
Section

6
. We begin with the following
Section
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
. Then

\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)=\left(F_{k}\psi\alpha,\psi\beta\right).

For this proof we write
\lambda=\psi\alpha
and
\mu=\psi\beta
. First we show that one side of the equation is non-zero if and only if the other is. We use the terminology and notation relating to the (bar)-abacus from the proofs of
6.1
,
6.2
,
6.3
and
6.4
; we use abacus displays with
l+1
beads for
\lambda
and
\mu
. We define functions

\displaystyle\iota:\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\bar{\iota}:\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\longmapsto b+(m-h)\left\lfloor\frac{b}{h}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\longmapsto b+(h-m)\left\lfloor\frac{b}{m}\right\rfloor.

Observe that
\iota
and
\bar{\iota}
restrict to mutually inverse bijections between
g+h\mathbb{Z}
and
g+m\mathbb{Z}
for each
0\leqslant g\leqslant n
, and that position
b
is occupied in
\operatorname{Ab}(\lambda)
if and only if position
\bar{\iota}(b)
is occupied in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
(and similarly for
\mu
and
\beta
).

Suppose first that
\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
. Then there are sets
D_{0},\dots,D_{h-1}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
D_{g}\subset g+h\mathbb{Z}
and
\lvert D_{n-g}\rvert+\lvert D_{n+g}\rvert=2k
for each
g
, such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\beta)
is obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
by making bead moves from
D_{n},D_{n+1}\cup D_{n-1},\dots,D_{h+1}\cup D_{0}
in turn. The assumption that
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
implies that
D_{n+g}=D_{n+g-1}+1
for
g=1,\dots,n
, and that
D_{0}=D_{h-1}+1
. So in fact
\lvert D_{g}\rvert=k
for each
g
. Now if we define
E_{g}=\iota(D_{g})
for
g=0,\dots,n
, then
E_{g}\subset g+m\mathbb{Z}
and
\lvert E_{g}\rvert=k
for each
g
, and
\operatorname{Ab}(\mu)
can be obtained from
\operatorname{Ab}(\lambda)
by making bead moves from
E_{n},E_{n-1},\dots,E_{0}
in turn. So
\left(F_{k}\lambda,\mu\right)\neq 0
.

The converse is very similar: if
\left(F_{k}\lambda,\mu\right)\neq 0
, then there are sets
E_{0},\dots,E_{n}
with the properties given above and satisfying
E_{0}=E_{n}+1
. Now if we define
D_{g}=\bar{\iota}(E_{g})
for
g=0,\dots,n
, and
D_{g}=D_{g-1}+1
for
g=n+1,\dots,h-1
, then the sets
D_{g}
have the properties in the above paragraph and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\beta)
is obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
by making bead moves from
D_{n},D_{n+1}\cup D_{n-1},\dots,D_{h-1}\cup D_{0}
. So
\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)\neq 0
.

So we assume that both sides of the equation are non-zero, and define the sets
D_{0},\dots,D_{h-1}
and
E_{0},\dots,E_{n}
as in the last two paragraphs. Also, for
g=1,\dots,n
let
F_{g}
be the set of parts
b
of
\alpha
congruent to
g
modulo
h
such that
b\notin D_{g}
; in other words,
F_{g}
is the set of positions of beads on runner
g
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
that are
not
moved in constructing
\beta
from
\alpha
. We define the partitions
\alpha^{(0)},\dots,\alpha^{(m)}
as in the proof of
6.4
, and the partitions
\lambda^{(0)},\dots,\lambda^{(m)}
as in the proof of
6.2
(using
E_{0},\dots,E_{n}
in place of
D_{0},\dots,D_{n}
) , so that

\displaystyle\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k)}\alpha,\alpha^{(1)}\right)\ \times\ \prod_{g=1}^{n}\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2k)}\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)}\right),

\displaystyle\left(F_{k}\lambda,\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{g=0}^{n}\left(\mathrm{f}_{-g-1-l}^{(k)}\lambda^{(g)},\lambda^{(g+1)}\right).

We will compare these two expressions term by term. First consider
\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n}^{(k)}\alpha,\alpha^{(1)}\right)=q^{4\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})}
and
\left(\mathrm{f}_{-1-l}^{(k)}\lambda,\lambda^{(1)}\right)=q^{2n(\lambda,\lambda^{(1)})}
. Since runner
n+1
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha)
is empty,
\alpha
has no
n
-bar-removable nodes, so

\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})=\lvert\left\{\left.(c,d)\ \right|\ \smash{c<d,\ c\in F_{n},\ d\in D_{n}}\right\}\rvert.

A similar statement applies for
n(\lambda,\lambda^{(1)})
, and the relationship between the abacus displays then gives
n(\lambda,\lambda^{(1)})=\check{n}(\alpha,\alpha^{(1)})
.

Now take
1\leqslant g\leqslant n-1
, and consider
\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{n-g}^{(2k)}\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)}\right)=q^{2\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})}
and
\left(\mathrm{f}_{-g-1-l}^{(k)}\lambda^{(g)},\lambda^{(g+1)}\right)=q^{2n(\lambda^{(g)},\lambda^{(g+1)})}
. By considering
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\alpha^{(g)})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\check{n}(\alpha^{(g)},\alpha^{(g+1)})=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g},\ d\in D_{n-g}\cup D_{n+g}\right.\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle-\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g+1},\ d\in D_{n-g}\cup D_{n+g}\right.\right\}\right\rvert.

(Note that the terms in the minuend with
c\in F_{n-g}\cap(F_{n-g+1}-1)
do not correspond to bar-addable nodes. But these terms cancel with the terms in the subtrahend with
c\in F_{n-g+1}\cap(F_{n-g}+1)
, which do not correspond to bar-removable nodes.) Doing the same calculation for
\lambda^{(g)}
and applying
\bar{\iota}
,

\displaystyle n(\lambda^{(g)},\lambda^{(g+1)})=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g},\ d\in D_{n-g}\right.\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle-\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g+1},\ d\in D_{n-g}\right.\right\}\right\rvert.

Finally, we look at
\left(\check{\mathrm{f}}_{0}^{(2k)}\alpha^{(n)},\beta\right)=q^{\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)}
and
\left(\mathrm{f}_{-l}^{(k)}\lambda^{(n)},\mu\right)=q^{2n(\lambda^{(n)},\beta)}
. Now there are two added nodes of bar-residue
0
corresponding to each element of
D_{h-1}
, two
0
-bar-removable nodes corresponding to each element of
F_{1}\setminus\{1\}
, and a
0
-bar-addable node in column
1
provided
1\notin F_{1}
. So

\displaystyle\check{n}(\alpha^{(n)},\beta)

\displaystyle=2k-4\left\lvert\left\{\left.(c,d)\ \right|\ \smash{c<d,\ c\in F_{1},\ d\in D_{h-1}}\right\}\right\rvert.

Correspondingly,

\displaystyle n(\lambda^{(n)},\mu)

\displaystyle=k-\left\lvert\left\{\left.(c,d)\ \right|\ \smash{c<d,\ c\in F_{1},\ d\in D_{h-1}}\right\}\right\rvert.

Combining these cases, we find that
\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)=q^{2s}\left(F_{k}\lambda,\mu\right)
, where

s=\sum_{g=1}^{n}\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g+1},\ d\in D_{n+g-1}\right.\right\}\right\rvert-\left\lvert\left\{\smash{(c,d)}\ \left|\ c<d,\ c\in F_{n-g+1},\ d\in D_{n+g}\right.\right\}\right\rvert.

But
D_{n+g}=D_{n+g-1}+1
for each
g
, so each summand is zero, and hence
\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)=\left(F_{k}\lambda,\mu\right)
.
∎

Now we are in a position to prove our main result comparing canonical basis elements, extending
5.2
. We consider the two sets of partitions

\left\{\left.\pi\|\alpha\ \right|\ \smash{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}},\ \pi\in\mathcal{P}}\right\},\qquad\left\{\left.\pi|\lambda\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l},\ \pi\in\mathcal{P}}\right\}.

If we let
\psi
be the bijection from
Section

4
, then there is a bijection between these two sets given by
\pi\|\alpha\mapsto\pi|\psi\alpha
. Our aim is to extend the linear map
\Psi
to give a correspondence between the canonical basis elements. The obvious map
\pi\|\lambda\mapsto\pi|\psi\alpha
does not work, because of the factor
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\rho}
appearing in
6.4
.

In fact, we need to restrict quite substantially the set of partitions that we consider. Suppose
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
. Given
u\geqslant 0
, say that the partition
\pi|\lambda
is
u
-super-separated
if
\rho|\lambda
is
u
-separated for every partition
\rho
with
\lvert\rho\rvert=\lvert\pi\rvert
. (Equivalently,
\pi|\lambda
is
u
-super-separated if it is
(u+\lvert\pi\rvert-\pi_{1})
-separated.)

Similarly, given
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
, say that
\pi\|\alpha
is
u
-super-bar-separated
if
\rho\|\alpha
is
u
-bar-separated for every
\rho
with
\lvert\rho\rvert=\lvert\pi\rvert
. (Equivalently,
\pi\|\alpha
is
u
-super-bar-separated if and only if
\pi|\psi\alpha
is
u
-super-separated.)

For the case
u=0
, we will simply say “super-separated” to mean “
0
-super-separated”, and similarly for super-bar-separated.

Our next step is to show how the
u
-super-separated condition is affected when we apply the operators
F_{k}
. The following is a variation on the final statement of
6.1
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l}
and
\pi,\rho\in\mathcal{P}
, and that
k>0
with
\left(F_{k}(\pi|\lambda),\rho|\mu\right)\neq 0
. If
\pi|\lambda
is
(k+u)
-super-separated for some
u\geqslant 0
, then
\rho|\mu
is
u
-super-separated.

The statement that
\pi|\lambda
is
(k+u)
-super-separated is the same as saying that
\pi|\lambda
is
(k+u+\lvert\pi\rvert-\pi_{1})
-separated. Since
\rho|\mu
appears with non-zero coefficient in
F_{k}(\pi|\lambda)
,
6.1
shows that
\sigma|\mu
does as well, where
\sigma
is the partition obtained by adding
\lvert\rho\rvert-\lvert\pi\rvert
nodes to
\pi
at the end of the first row. So from the final statement of
6.1
,
\sigma|\mu
is
(u+\lvert\pi\rvert-\pi_{1})
-separated, which is the same as
(u+\lvert\sigma\rvert-\sigma_{1})
-separated, so
\sigma|\mu
is
u
-super-separated, and therefore
\rho|\mu
is
u
-super-separated.
∎

Now for each
u
define

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSP}_{u}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left.\pi|\lambda\ \right|\ \smash{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{+}_{\leqslant l},\ \pi\in\mathcal{P},\ \pi|\lambda\text{ $u$-super-separated }}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\operatorname{BSSP}_{u}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left.\pi\|\alpha\ \right|\ \smash{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}},\ \pi\in\mathcal{P},\ \pi\|\alpha\text{ $u$-super-bar-separated }}\right\rangle.

We write
\operatorname{SSP}
and
\operatorname{BSSP}
for
\operatorname{SSP}_{0}
and
\operatorname{BSSP}_{0}
.

Lemma

7.2
shows that
F_{k}
maps
\operatorname{SSP}_{k+u}
to
\operatorname{SSP}_{u}
. Similarly,
\check{F}_{k}
maps
\operatorname{BSSP}_{k+u}
to
\operatorname{BSSP}_{u}
. To extend
7.1
to connect the actions of
F_{k}
and
\check{F}_{k}
, we need to define a linear bijection between
\operatorname{BSSP}
and
\operatorname{SSP}
. Recalling the Kostka polynomials
K_{\rho\pi}(t)
from
Section

3
, we define

\Theta:\operatorname{BSSP}\longrightarrow\operatorname{SSP}

by setting

\Theta(\pi\|\alpha)=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{P}}K_{\rho\pi}(-q^{2})\rho|\psi\alpha

and extending linearly. (
\Theta
is bijective because the matrix of Kostka polynomials
K_{\rho\pi}(t)
for
\rho,\pi\in\mathcal{P}(r)
is invertible for each
r
.)

Now we can reconcile
6.2
and
6.4
via the following
Section
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose
v\in\operatorname{BSSP}_{k}
. Then
\Theta(\check{F}_{k}v)=F_{k}\Theta(v)
.

By linearity we can assume
v=\pi\|\alpha
for some
k
-super-bar-separated partition
\pi\|\alpha
. Since both sides of the equation lie in
\operatorname{SSP}
, it suffices to show that

\left(\Theta(\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha)),\sigma|\mu\right)=\left(F_{k}\Theta(\pi\|\alpha),\sigma|\mu\right)

for each separated partition
\sigma|\mu
.

We use the results of
Section

3
, with the variable
t
specialised to
-q^{2}
. So we let
P_{\lambda},Q_{\lambda}
be the two types of Hall–Littlewood symmetric functions defined for
t=-q^{2}
, and
\langle\,,\,\rangle
the Hall–Littlewood inner product.

Now

\displaystyle\left(\Theta(\check{F}_{k}(\pi\|\alpha)),\sigma|\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\tau\end{subarray}}\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\tau}\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\beta\right)\left(\Theta\left(\tau\|\beta\right),\sigma|\mu\right)

(by
6.4
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\tau\end{subarray}}K_{\sigma\tau}(-q^{2})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\tau}\left(\check{F}_{k}\alpha,\psi^{-1}\mu\right)

(from the definition of
\Theta
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{P}\\
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\tau\end{subarray}}\langle s_{\sigma},Q_{\tau}\rangle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi\tau}\left(F_{k}\psi\alpha,\mu\right)

(by
7.1
and the definition of the coefficients
K_{\sigma\tau}(t)
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}}\langle s_{\sigma},Q_{\tau}\rangle\langle\partial_{(r)}P_{\tau},Q_{\pi}\rangle\left(F_{k}\psi\alpha,\mu\right)

(by
3.2
)

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{(r)}s_{\sigma},Q_{\pi}\rangle\left(F_{k}\psi\alpha,\mu\right)

(since
s_{\sigma}=\sum_{\tau}\langle s_{\sigma},Q_{\tau}\rangle P_{\tau}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{P}\\
\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\sigma\end{subarray}}\langle s_{\rho},Q_{\pi}\rangle\left(F_{k}\psi\alpha,\mu\right)

(by the Pieri rule for Schur functions)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{P}}K_{\rho\pi}(-q^{2})\left(F_{k}\rho|\psi\alpha,\sigma|\mu\right)

(by
6.2
)

\displaystyle=\left(F_{k}\Theta(\pi\|\alpha),\sigma|\mu\right)

(from the definition of
\Theta
).∎

Now we want to use
7.3
to compare canonical basis elements. Given partitions
\pi,\rho
, let’s write
\rho\succcurlyeq\pi
if either
\lvert\rho\rvert<\lvert\pi\rvert
or
\rho\trianglerighteqslant\pi
. Part (2) of the next
Section
is our main result comparing canonical bases.

Theorem 7.4
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
and
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
, and that
\pi\|\alpha
is
u
-super-bar-separated for some
u\geqslant 0
. Then:

(1)

\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)
is a linear combination of basis elements
\rho\|\beta
in which
\rho\succcurlyeq\pi
and
\rho\|\beta
is
u
-super-bar-separated;

(2)

\Theta(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha))=\mathrm{G}_{m}(\pi|\psi\alpha)
.

We proceed by induction on
\pi
, using the order
\succcurlyeq
. The case
\pi=\varnothing
follows from
5.2
, so assume
\pi\neq\varnothing
, and that the theorem is true if
\pi
is replaced by any
\rho
with
\rho\succ\pi
.

Let
k
be the last non-zero part of
\pi
, let
\pi^{-}
denote the partition obtained by deleting this last part, and consider the bar-invariant vector
V=\check{F}_{k}\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi^{-}\|\alpha)
. Since
\pi^{-}\|\alpha
is
(k+u)
-super-bar-separated, the inductive hypothesis says that
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi^{-}\|\alpha)
is a linear combination of basis elements
\sigma\|\gamma
for which
\sigma\succ\pi^{-}
and
\sigma\|\gamma
is
(k+u)
-super-bar-separated. Now
6.3
shows that the terms
\rho\|\beta
appearing in
V
all have the properties that
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
for some
r\leqslant k
and
\rho\|\beta
is
u
-super-bar-separated. If
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
with either
\lvert\sigma\rvert<\lvert\pi^{-}\rvert
or
r<k
, then
\lvert\rho\rvert<\lvert\pi\rvert
. On the other hand, if
\sigma\trianglerighteqslant\pi^{-}
and
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\leadsto}}\rho
, then
\rho\trianglerighteqslant\pi
by
Lemma

2.2
, with equality only if
\sigma=\pi^{-}
.

So
V
is a linear combination of terms
\rho\|\beta
with
\rho\succcurlyeq\pi
. Moreover,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\pi^{-}\pi}=1
, so for any
\beta
the coefficient of
\pi\|\beta
in
V
is the same as the coefficient of
\pi^{-}\|\beta
in
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi^{-}\|\alpha)
. This means in particular that
\pi\|\alpha
occurs with coefficient
1
, and that for any
\beta\neq\alpha
the coefficient of
\pi\|\beta
in
V
is divisible by
q
.

Because
V
is bar-invariant, we can write it as a linear combination of canonical basis vectors. The properties of
V
described in the last paragraph and the inductive hypothesis mean that when we do this, the canonical basis vector
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)
must occur with coefficient
1
. So we can write

V=\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)+\sum_{\beta,\rho}a_{\beta\rho}\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\rho\|\beta),

with the sum being over pairs
\beta,\rho
for which
\rho\succ\pi
and
\rho\|\beta
is
k
-super-bar-separated, and each coefficient
a_{\beta\rho}
lies in
\mathbb{C}(q+q^{-1})
. Now property (1) for
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)
follows from the inductive hypothesis and the corresponding property for
V
shown above.

Applying
\Theta
to both sides of (*) and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\check{F}_{k}\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi^{-}\|\alpha)\right)-\sum_{\beta,\rho}a_{\beta\rho}\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\rho\|\beta)\right)

\displaystyle=F_{k}\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi^{-}\|\alpha)\right)-\sum_{\beta,\rho}a_{\beta\rho}\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\rho\|\beta)\right)

(by
7.3
)

\displaystyle=F_{k}\mathrm{G}_{m}(\pi^{-}\|\psi\alpha)-\sum_{\beta,\rho}a_{\beta\rho}\mathrm{G}_{m}(\rho|\psi\beta)

(by the inductive hypothesis).

This means in particular that
\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)\right)
is bar-invariant. Moreover,
\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)\right)
equals
\pi\|\alpha
plus a linear combination of other standard basis vectors with coefficients divisible by
q
; this comes from the corresponding property for
\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)
, the definition of
\Theta
and the fact (
Lemma

3.4
) that the inverse Kostka matrix
K^{-1}(-q^{2})
is unitriangular with off-diagonal entries divisible by
q^{2}
. So the defining properties of the canonical basis mean that
\Theta\left(\check{\mathrm{G}}_{h}(\pi\|\alpha)\right)=\mathrm{G}_{m}(\pi|\psi\alpha)
.
∎

Example
.

7.4
in weight spaces which are completely contained within
\operatorname{BSSP}
, so we give an example to illustrate this situation. Take
m=3
, and consider the weight space in
\mathcal{F}
spanned by partitions with
3

(2^{2},1^{2})
and
3

3

\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
).

The matrices in
Figures

1
and
2
are almost identical, but differ because of the effect of the polynomials
K^{-1}_{\rho\pi}(-q^{2})
. To see the effect of
7.4
, consider for example the fourth and fifth rows of the two matrices. In the first matrix, these rows are labelled by the partitions
(1^{2})|(4,3,1^{2})
and
(2)|(4,3,1^{2})
. The inverse Kostka polynomials
K^{-1}_{\rho\pi}(-q^{2})
for partitions of
2
are given by the following matrix.

\begin{array}[]{c|cc|}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(1^{2})$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(2)$}\\
\hline\cr(1^{2})&1&\cdot\\
(2)&q^{2}&1\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Hence the linear map
\Theta^{-1}
sends

\displaystyle(1^{2})|(4,3,1^{2})\longmapsto\,

\displaystyle(1^{2})\|\psi(4,3,1^{2})+q^{2}(2)\|\psi(4,3,1^{2})

\displaystyle=(16,12,7,6,5^{2},2,1)+q^{2}(16,12,10,7,6,2,1)

\displaystyle(2)|(4,3,1^{2})\longmapsto\,

\displaystyle(2)\|\psi(4,3,1^{2})

\displaystyle=(16,12,10,7,6,2,1).

As a consequence, the fifth row of the matrix in
Figure

2
is obtained from the fifth row of the matrix in
Figure

1
by adding
q^{2}
times the fourth row. A similar calculation applies to the first three rows of the matrices (in fact, the
3\times 3
matrix at the top right of the matrix in
Figure

2
is precisely the matrix of polynomials
K^{-1}_{\rho\pi}(-q^{2})
for partitions of
3
).

8
Equivalences on weight spaces and Rouquier cores

In the representation theory of the symmetric group, equivalences between blocks of the same defect (starting from the work of Scopes) have proved to be an important tool in studying the representation theory of symmetric groups. One of the Scopes equivalence classes of blocks was identified by Rouquier as being of particular interest. These blocks are now commonly known as
Rouquier blocks
or
RoCK blocks
, and (in combination with Scopes’s equivalences) have been instrumental in proving various conjectures, most significantly in the proof of Broué’s abelian defect group conjecture for the symmetric groups
[
CK
,
CR
]
. There is a parallel theory for blocks of double covers of symmetric groups, but with substantial additional complications. We will describe this in more detail in
Section

9
, but in this section we describe analogous results in
\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\mathcal{F}}
: we define the notion of
extremal
weight spaces in
\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\mathcal{F}}
, we recall the formula (due to Chuang–Tan and Leclerc–Miyachi) for the canonical bases in extremal weight spaces in type
A^{(1)}_{n}
, and we use this (and our main theorem from
Section

7
) to give a corresponding formula for
\check{\mathcal{F}}
.

8.1
Extremal weight spaces in type
A^{(1)}_{m-1}

For each
m
-core
\nu
and each
w\geqslant 0
, let
\mathcal{P}^{\nu,w}
denote the set of partitions with
m
-core
\nu
and
m
-weight
w
, and let
W^{\nu,w}
denote the weight space in
\mathcal{F}
spanned by
\mathcal{P}^{\nu,w}
. If
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that two weight spaces
W^{\nu,w}
and
W^{\xi,w}
form a
[w:k]
-pair
of residue
i
if
\xi
is obtained from
\nu
by adding
k
nodes of residue
i
. This is equivalent to saying that
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{k}
maps
W^{\nu,w}
to
W^{\xi,w}
. We will write
V\preccurlyeq W
to mean that
V
and
W
form a
[w:k]
-pair for some
k
, and extend
\preccurlyeq
transitively to give a partial order on the set of weight spaces of
m
-weight
w
. If
V
and
W
form a
[w:k]
-pair of residue
i
with
k\geqslant w
, we say that
V
and
W
are
Scopes equivalent
; this is equivalent to the condition that
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(k)}
and
\mathrm{e}_{i}^{(k)}
map the canonical basis for
V
to the canonical basis for
W
and vice versa. Extending this relation transitively defines an equivalence relation (the Scopes equivalence) on the set of weight spaces of
m
-weight
w
, with only finitely many equivalence classes. The partial order
\preccurlyeq
on weight spaces descends to a partial order on Scopes classes, and there is a unique maximal class for this order. We call the weight spaces in this class the
extremal
weight spaces in
\mathcal{F}
; these are precisely the weight spaces corresponding to RoCK blocks of symmetric groups. To describe the weight spaces in this class, we introduce the notion of a Rouquier core.

Take
w\geqslant 1
, and let
\nu
be an
m
-core. Say that
\nu
is
w
-Rouquier
if it has an abacus display (with
s
beads, say) in which there are at least
w-1
more beads on runner
i
than on runner
i-1
, for
i=1,\dots,m-1
. If
\nu
is
w
-Rouquier, then
W_{\nu,w}
is an example of an extremal weight space.

To describe the canonical basis coefficients in extremal weight spaces, we need to recall the notion of the
m
-quotient of a partition. Suppose
\nu
is
w
-Rouquier with an
s
-bead abacus display as above. By increasing
s
by a multiple of
m
if necessary, we can assume there are at least
w
beads on each runner. Now given
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\nu,w}
, we can construct the abacus display for
\lambda
with
s
beads from the abacus display for
\nu
by moving some beads down their runners. We define the
m
-quotient

(\lambda(0),\dots,\lambda(m-1))
to be the
m
-tuple of partitions in which
\lambda(i)
is the partition obtained by viewing runner
i
in isolation as
1
-runner abacus; more precisely,
\lambda(i)_{r}
is the number of empty spaces above the
r
th lowest bead on runner
i
.

(In fact the definition of
m
-quotient we have given is not the most usual one, in which the indices
i
may be permuted cyclically according to the number of beads on the abacus. The
m
-quotient defined here is called the
ordered
m
-quotient
in
[
F2
]
.)

Now we can give the main result of Chuang–Tan and Leclerc–Miyachi (translated to the conventions we are using). Recall that
\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}
denotes the coefficient of
\lambda
in
\mathrm{G}_{m}(\mu)
. In the following theorem,
\operatorname{c}^{\lambda}_{\sigma\tau}
denotes a Littlewood–Richardson coefficient, which should be interpreted as
0
if
\lvert\lambda\rvert\neq\lvert\sigma\rvert+\lvert\tau\rvert
.

Theorem 8.1

(
[
CT1
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
LM
, Corollary 10]
).

Suppose
\nu
is a
w
-Rouquier
m
-core, and
\lambda,\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{\nu,w}
. Then
\mu
is
m
-restricted if and only if
\mu(m-1)=\varnothing
. If
\mu
is
m
-restricted, then

\operatorname{d}_{\lambda\mu}=\left(\sum\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\operatorname{c}^{\lambda(i)}_{\sigma(i)\tau(i)}\operatorname{c}^{\mu(i-1)}_{\sigma(i-1)\tau(i)^{\prime}}\right)q^{2\sum_{i}i(\lvert\lambda(i)\rvert-\lvert\mu(i)\rvert)},

where the sum is over all partitions
\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(m-2),\tau(1),\dots,\tau(m-1)
, and
\sigma(0)
and
\sigma(m-1)
should be read as
\lambda(0)
and
\varnothing
respectively.

8.2
Extremal weight spaces in type
A^{(2)}_{h-1}

Now we describe the corresponding theory in type
A^{(2)}_{h-1}
. If
\gamma
is an
h
-bar-core and
w\geqslant 0
, we let
\check{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma,w}
denote the set of
h
-strict partitions with
h
-bar-core
\gamma
and
h
-bar-weight
w
, and we let
\check{W}^{\gamma,w}
denote the weight space in
\check{\mathcal{F}}
spanned by
\check{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma,w}
. The notion of a
[w:k]
-pair of weight spaces of bar-residue
i
, and the partial order
\preccurlyeq
are defined exactly as in type
A^{(1)}
. Two weight spaces
\check{W}^{\gamma,w}
and
\check{W}^{\delta,w}
forming a
[w:k]
-pair of bar-residue
i
are
Scopes–Kessar equivalent
if the divided powers
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{(k)}
and
\mathrm{e}_{i}^{(k)}
map the canonical basis of
\check{W}^{\gamma,w}
to the canonical basis of
\check{W}^{\delta,w}
and vice versa. (The exact condition in terms of
w,k,i
for a Scopes–Kessar equivalence is given in
[
F4
, Theorem 4.6]
, but we do not need this here). As in type
A^{(1)}
, we extend this relation to give an equivalence relation on the set of weight spaces with
h
-bar-weight
w
, and the order
\preccurlyeq
descends to an order on equivalence classes. Again, there is a unique maximal class, whose elements we call extremal weight spaces.

To give examples of extremal weight spaces, we introduce Rouquier bar-cores, following Kleshchev and Livesey
[
KlL
, §4.1a]
. Say that an
h
-bar-core
\gamma
is
w
-Rouquier
if in the bar-abacus display for
\gamma
there are at least
w
beads on runner
1
, and at least
w-1
more beads on runner
i
than on runner
i-1
, for
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
. If
\gamma
is
w
-Rouquier, then the weight space
\check{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma,w}
is extremal.

We can use the results in this paper to find the canonical basis coefficients in extremal weight spaces. Suppose
\gamma
is a
w
-Rouquier
h
-bar-core, and let
l
be the length of
\gamma
. Then automatically
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
(because
\gamma
has parts congruent to
1,2,\dots,n
modulo
h
only), and if we apply the function
\psi
, we obtain a
w
-Rouquier
m
-core.

To give a formula analogous to
8.1
, we need to define
h
-bar-quotients. Given
\alpha\in\check{\mathcal{P}}^{\gamma,w}
, let
\alpha(0)
be the partition obtained by taking all the parts of
\alpha
divisible by
h
and dividing them all by
h
. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, define
\alpha(i)
by letting
\alpha(i)_{r}
be the number of empty spaces above the
r
th lowest bead on runner
i
in the bar-abacus display for
\alpha
. (We remark that the particular version of
h
-bar-quotient we have defined here is specific to partitions with Rouquier cores; other versions
[
MY
,
Y
]
have been defined which apply to all
h
-strict partitions.) In particular, we can write
\alpha=\alpha(0)\|\beta
, where
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\operatorname{std}}
, and
\alpha(0)|\psi\beta
is the partition with
m
-core
\psi\gamma
and
m
-quotient
(\alpha(0),\dots,\alpha(n))
. Moreover, the
w
-Rouquier condition implies that
\alpha
is bar-separated: the last bead on runner
0
of the bar-abacus display is in position
h\alpha(0)_{1}
, while the first empty position not on runner
0
is position
1+(d-{\alpha(1)}^{\prime}_{1})h
or later, where
1+dh
is the position of the last bead on runner
1
for
\alpha
. Since
\alpha(0)_{1}+{\alpha(1)}^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant w\leqslant d
, the bar-separated condition holds.

Example
.

Suppose
h=5
and
w=4
, and let
\gamma=(32,27,22,17,16,12,11,7,6,2,1)
. Then
\gamma
is a
4
-Rouquier
5
-bar-core, and
\psi\gamma=(9,7,5,3^{2},2^{2},1^{2})
is a
4
-Rouquier
3
-core.

Now we can apply
7.4
and
8.1
, and we obtain the following result giving the canonical bases for weight spaces with Rouquier bar-cores. (This generalises in a straightforward way to give the canonical bases for all extremal weight spaces.)

Theorem 8.2
.

Suppose
\gamma
is a
w
-Rouquier
h
-bar-core, and that
\alpha
and
\beta
are
h
-strict partitions with
h
-bar-core
\gamma
and
h
-bar-weight
w
. Then
\beta
is restricted if and only if
\beta(n)=\varnothing
. If
\beta
is restricted, then

\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\sum K^{-1}_{\alpha(0)\sigma(0)}(-q^{2})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{c}^{\alpha(i)}_{\sigma(i)\tau(i)}\operatorname{c}^{\beta(i-1)}_{\sigma(i-1)\tau(i)^{\prime}}\right)q^{2\sum_{i}i(\lvert\alpha(i)\rvert-\lvert\beta(i)\rvert)},

where the sum is over all partitions
\sigma(0),\dots,\sigma(n-1),\tau(1),\dots,\tau(n)
, and
\sigma(n)
should be read as
\varnothing
.

Example
.

Take
h=3
and
w=4
. The
3
-bar-core
(10,7,4,1)
is
4
-Rouquier, and the canonical basis in the corresponding weight space of
3
-bar-weight
4
is given by the matrix in
Figure

3
.

\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc|}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(10,7,4,3^{4},1)$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(10,7,6,4,3^{2},1)$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(10,7,6^{2},4,1)$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(10,9,7,4,3,1)$}&\rotatebox{90.0}{$(12,10,7,4,1)$}\\
\hline\cr(10,7,4,3^{4},1)&1&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
(10,7,6,4,3^{2},1)&q^{6}-q^{4}+q^{2}&1&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
(10,7,6^{2},4,1)&-q^{6}&q^{2}&1&\cdot&\cdot\\
(10,9,7,4,3,1)&-q^{6}+q^{4}&q^{2}&q^{2}&1&\cdot\\
(12,10,7,4,1)&q^{6}&q^{4}&\cdot&q^{2}&1\\
(13,7,4,3^{3},1)&q^{2}&q^{2}&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
(13,7,6,4,3,1)&-q^{6}+q^{4}&-q^{6}+q^{4}+q^{2}&q^{2}&q^{2}&\cdot\\
(13,9,7,4,1)&q^{6}&q^{6}+q^{4}&q^{4}&q^{4}+q^{2}&q^{2}\\
(13,10,4,3^{2},1)&\cdot&q^{4}&\cdot&q^{4}&\cdot\\
(13,10,6,4,1)&\cdot&q^{6}&q^{4}&q^{6}+q^{4}&q^{4}\\
(16,7,4,3^{2},1)&q^{4}&q^{4}&q^{4}&\cdot&\cdot\\
(16,7,6,4,1)&q^{6}&q^{6}+q^{4}&q^{6}&q^{4}&\cdot\\
(13,10,7,3,1)&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&q^{6}&q^{6}\\
(16,10,4,3,1)&\cdot&q^{6}&q^{6}&q^{6}&\cdot\\
(19,7,4,3,1)&q^{6}&q^{6}&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
(13,10,7,4)&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&q^{8}\\
(16,10,7,1)&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&q^{8}&\cdot\\
(16,13,4,1)&\cdot&\cdot&q^{8}&\cdot&\cdot\\
(19,10,4,1)&\cdot&q^{8}&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
(22,7,4,1)&q^{8}&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&\cdot\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 3:
Canonical basis in an extremal weight space

As an example of how entries are calculated, consider the
(\alpha,\beta)
-entry where
\alpha=(13,7,6,4,3,1)
and
\beta=(10,7,6,4,3^{2},1)
. In this case

(\alpha(0),\alpha(1))=((2,1),(1)),\qquad(\beta(0),\beta(1))=((2,1^{2}),\varnothing),

giving
\sum_{i}i(\lvert\alpha(i)\rvert-\lvert\beta(i)\rvert)=1
. The Littlewood–Richardson coefficient
\operatorname{c}^{(2,1^{2})}_{\sigma(1)}
equals
1
if
\sigma=(1^{3})
or
(2,1)
, and
0
otherwise. So

\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}=(K^{-1}_{(2,1)(1^{3})}(-q^{2})+K^{-1}_{(2,1)(2,1)}(-q^{2}))q^{2}=((q^{2}-q^{4})+1)q^{2}.

9
RoCK blocks of symmetric groups and their double covers

We end this paper by explaining the connections between our results and the spin representation theory of the symmetric group, giving conjectural decomposition numbers for RoCK blocks with abelian defect.

We refer to the book by Kleshchev
[
Kl
]
for background on spin representation theory of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, but we summarise the main points here. As in the introduction, let
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
denote a proper double cover of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We consider representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
over an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{F}
of odd characteristic
h
. The non-trivial central element
z\in\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
acts as
\pm 1
on any irreducible module; modules on which
z
acts as
-1
are called
spin modules
. A block of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is called a spin block if it contains a spin module (in which case all the irreducible modules in the block are spin modules).

In practice it is more convenient to work with
\mathbb{F}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
as a superalgebra, and consider supermodules. So we will consider spin superblocks (which coincide with spin blocks except in the trivial case of blocks of defect
0
). For each strict partition
\alpha
of
n
(i.e. each partition in which the positive parts are distinct) there is an irreducible spin supermodule for
\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, and these modules give all the irreducible spin supermodules for
\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. We let
\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}
denote an
h
-modular reduction of this module. For each restricted
h
-strict partition
\beta
of
n
, there is an irreducible spin supermodule
\mathbf{D}^{\beta}
for
\mathbb{F}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, and these modules give all the irreducible spin supermodules for
\mathbb{F}\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. So (apart from the distinction between modules and supermodules) the decomposition number problem for spin representations of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
asks for the composition multiplicities
[\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}:\mathbf{D}^{\beta}]
for all
\alpha,\beta
.

The block classification for
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
(due to Humphreys
[
Hu
]
) says that
\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\beta}
lie in the same superblock of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
have the same
h
-bar-core (in which case they have the same
h
-bar-weight as well). In particular, we can talk about the
h
-bar-core and
h
-bar-weight of a superblock, and there is a direct correspondence between spin superblocks of
\hat{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
(as
n
varies) and weight spaces in the Fock space
\check{\mathcal{F}}
. Moreover, the action of the generators
\check{\mathrm{e}}_{i},\check{\mathrm{f}}_{i}
on standard basis elements in
\check{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to the branching rules describing induction and restriction of the modules
\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}
. This led Leclerc and Thibon to draw connections between canonical basis coefficients and decomposition numbers. To give a statement of their conjecture, we introduce some more notation. Given an
h
-strict partition
\alpha
of
n
, we can define in a combinatorial way a restricted
h
-strict partition called the
regularisation

\alpha^{\operatorname{reg}}
of
\alpha
; this was introduced by Brundan and Kleshchev, who showed that the decomposition number
[\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}:\mathbf{D}^{\alpha^{\operatorname{reg}}}]
is non-zero (giving its value explicitly), and that
\alpha^{\operatorname{reg}}
is the most dominant partition with this property. Now given any restricted
h
-strict partition
\beta
of
n
, define the
divided decomposition number

D_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{[\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}:\mathbf{D}^{\beta}]}{[\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}:\mathbf{D}^{\alpha^{\operatorname{reg}}}]}.

Then the Leclerc–Thibon conjecture on spin decomposition numbers states that
D_{\alpha\beta}
is simply the evaluation of
\check{\operatorname{d}}_{\alpha\beta}
at
q=1
when
n<h^{2}
. A natural extension of this conjecture (analogous to the James conjecture for the symmetric groups) would weaken the condition
n<p^{2}
to include all cases where
\alpha
and
\beta
have
h
-bar-weight less than
h
(this condition corresponds to the block containing
\mathbf{S}^{\alpha}
having abelian defect groups). As mentioned in the introduction, this conjecture is known to be false (in fact it predicts negative decomposition numbers!) but it is nevertheless true in many cases; in particular, in all blocks of abelian defect for which the decomposition numbers are explicitly known, the Leclerc–Thibon conjecture holds.

Now define a
RoCK block
to be a superblock with
h
-bar-weight
w
whose
h
-bar-core is
w
-Rouquier. Applying
8.2
, we make the following explicit conjecture. Here we write
K^{-1}_{\alpha\beta}
for the specialisation of
K^{-1}_{\alpha\beta}(t)
at
t=-1
. We continue to write
n=\frac{1}{2}(h-1)
.

Conjecture 9.1
.

Suppose
\gamma
is a
w
-Rouquier
h
-bar-core, and that
\alpha
is a strict partition and
\beta
a restricted
h
-strict partition, both with
h
-bar-core
\gamma
and
h
-bar-weight
w<h
. Define the
h
-bar-quotients
(\alpha(0),\dots,\alpha(n))
and
(\beta(0),\dots,\beta(n))
as above. Then

D_{\alpha\beta}=\sum K^{-1}_{\alpha(0)\sigma(0)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{c}^{\alpha(i)}_{\sigma(i)\tau(i)}\operatorname{c}^{\beta(i-1)}_{\sigma(i-1)\tau(i)^{\prime}}.

where the sum is over all partitions
\sigma(0),\dots,\sigma(n-1),\tau(1),\dots,\tau(n)
, and
\sigma(n)
should be read as
\varnothing
.

Recent work of Kleshchev and Livesey
[
KlL
]
studies RoCK blocks in detail, particularly in the abelian defect case. In a forthcoming paper
[
FKM
]
, we will use these results and the results of the present paper to prove
9.1
.
